Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

Executive Summary
During summer 2026, BoR may implement temperature-constrained operations at GCD to limit
downstream water temperatures and reduce conditions favorable to invasive smallmouth bass (Mi-
cropterus dolomieu). These operations require the use of bypass releases when necessary, thereby
reducing hydropower generation. This report quantifies the potential generation impacts of the an-
ticipated 2026 bypass season. The analysis assumes RM15, located roughly 30 river-miles down-
stream of the dam, as the compliance location and a daily flow-weighted average water-temperature
threshold of 15.5 ◦ C.
Four co-equal analysis periods are evaluated, defined by the combinations of two start dates (July 1
and July 10, 2026) and two end dates (October 1 and October 20, 2026). These periods bracket the
range of implementation dates under consideration and are described in Section 1.3.
The analysis evaluates 30 hydrological traces spanning the 1991–2020 historical record, each
solved as an independent dispatch optimization that maximizes the net benefit of GCD generation
to WAPA’s customers subject to the temperature constraint at RM15. Percentile conventions used
throughout this report are defined in Section 6.1.1 Six traces are identified as the closest analogs
to expected 2026 conditions under the 24MS Most Probable scenario through a multi-variable dis-
tance algorithm incorporating monthly elevation, inflow, and release data (X2011, X2015, X2014,
X2007, X2008, X2001). Each analysis period is evaluated across the four windows; all 30 traces
are projected to require bypass operations beyond the October 20 cutoff, and the extended-horizon
analysis quantifies the excluded generation loss for the portion of the season falling after the cut-
off. The full simulation spans June 1 through November 30, and bypass operations approximately
begin on June 9 (median biological onset across the 30-trace ensemble). The analysis addresses
both the energy dimension (cumulative GWh of bypass generation loss) and the capacity dimen-
sion (instantaneous MW reduction during peak demand hours), as each informs a different aspect
of resource planning.
Table 2 summarizes the median (P50) statistics across all four analysis periods; the full percentile
distribution (P10–P90) is provided in Table 5.1. The median bypass generation loss ranges from
245.9 GWh for the shortest period (July 10–October 1, 84 days) to 319.1 GWh for the longest
(July 1–October 20, 112 days). The P10–P90 uncertainty band is approximately 20–40 GWh
wide depending on the period, corresponding to a coefficient of variation of approximately 5%,
confirming that bypass generation loss is highly predictable once the analysis period is fixed. The
choice of end date has a larger impact than the choice of start date: delaying the end of bypass
operations from October 1 to October 20 adds approximately 54 GWh of generation loss (median),
while delaying the start from July 1 to July 10 reduces it by approximately 19 GWh.

Bypass requirements follow a characteristic seasonal arc driven by penstock temperatures, with
intensity peaking during late July through early September. Penstock temperature, the dominant
driver of bypass requirements, and the PCF conversion factor act together to determine bypass
generation loss. Figure 1 provides an executive dashboard of key metrics with the six analog traces
highlighted, and Figure 2 presents the seasonal narrative across the ensemble.
The capacity reduction analysis reveals a strong asymmetry across demand tiers: the dispatch op-
timization concentrates bypass in off-peak and mid-tier hours to preserve peak-hour generation.
During August peak hours, capacity reduction exceeds 100 MW in 57.9% of trace-hours and ex-
ceeds 150 MW in 28.1% of trace-hours across the pooled ensemble. The marketable capacity

Chapter 1

Introduction

1.1 Background and Regulatory Context
The Glen Canyon Dam (GCD), located on the Colorado River in northern Arizona, impounds
Lake Powell, the second-largest reservoir in the United States. It serves as the principal hydro-
electric facility within the Western Area Power Administration (WAPA) Colorado River Storage
Project (CRSP). The dam releases water through two distinct pathways. The penstock pathway
routes water through the powerplant turbines, generating electricity; the penstock draws water
from depth, and its release temperature reflects the reservoir’s vertical thermal structure, typically
ranging from approximately 9 to 12◦ C over the summer season. The bypass pathway routes water
through the river outlet works (bypass tubes), producing no electricity but delivering water drawn
from a greater depth with generally colder temperatures. These two pathways and the difference
in the temperatures of the water they carry are central to the operational tradeoff examined in this
report.
Smallmouth Bass (SMB) is an invasive species that poses a significant threat to native fish popula-
tions in the Colorado River below GCD. SMB had been occasionally detected in very low numbers
below GCD for roughly two decades, but the major ecological concern began in summer 2022,
when young-of-year fish were detected below the dam, suggesting possible reproduction, though
entrainment through the dam is an alternative explanation. As a warm-water invasive species, SMB
benefit from the recent increase in the Colorado River water temperatures, which has created more
favorable conditions for spawning and successful reproduction below GCD [11]. Temperature
is the primary management lever available to dam operators: maintaining sufficiently cold water
temperatures downstream of the dam inhibits SMB spawning and establishment. The Long-Term
Experimental and Management Plan (LTEMP) Record of Decision (ROD) provides the regulatory
framework for experimental and management actions at GCD, including temperature management
operations designed to protect native fish [5]. The 2024 LTEMP Supplemental Environmental Im-

 5

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

pact Statement (SEIS) Record of Decision further authorized temperature management operations
targeting invasive species control [10]. Under this framework, compliance is assessed against a
daily flow-weighted average temperature threshold of 15.5◦ C at a designated downstream location.
For summer 2026, the Department of the Interior selected RM15, a point located 30 river-miles
downstream of GCD, as the compliance location for temperature management operations. At this
location, downstream warming from solar radiation, air-water heat exchange, and tributary inflows
is moderate relative to more distant compliance points, but the short travel distance limits the ther-
mal budget available for warm penstock releases. The fundamental operational tradeoff is direct:
routing water through the bypass tubes maintains temperature compliance at RM15 but forgoes the
hydroelectric generation that the same water would have produced through the penstocks. Quanti-
fying this tradeoff, in terms of both energy (GWh) and capacity (MW), across a range of plausible
hydrological conditions is the central objective of this report.

1.2 Objective
This report quantifies the cost in generation loss of maintaining temperature compliance at RM15
across 30 hydrological traces spanning the 1991–2020 historical record. The analysis is operator-
facing: it characterizes what WAPA can expect in terms of reduced generation and capacity during
the summer 2026 bypass season, and it provides the analytical basis for sizing replacement power
needs. Both the energy dimension (cumulative GWh of bypass generation loss over the season) and
the capacity dimension (instantaneous MW reduction during specific demand hours) are reported,
as each informs different aspects of WAPA’s planning. Energy totals are relevant for seasonal
accounting and resource adequacy, while capacity reductions bear on real-time dispatch and firm
commitment obligations.
The analysis addresses four principal questions. First, what is the total generation impact of RM15
compliance, expressed as bypass generation loss in GWh and capacity reduction in MW, across
the range of plausible hydrological conditions? Second, at what point during the bypass season
is the impact on generation most severe, and how does it vary by month and demand tier? Third,
what thermal and hydrological conditions have the largest impact (penstock temperature, reservoir
elevation, bypass thermal effectiveness, or release volume)? Fourth, what level of replacement
power capacity and energy may WAPA need to procure, and at what percentile levels, to cover the
shortfall created by bypass operations?
Six analog traces were selected as the hydrologic scenarios most similar to the projected May
2026 24MS (Run ID 3317) Most Probable scenario [3]. The traces were identified using a mul-
tivariable distance algorithm based on monthly reservoir elevation, inflow, and release data, as
described in Section 2.5. These selected traces provide near-term planning context for the most
operationally relevant conditions, while the full 30-trace ensemble supports replacement-power
sizing and broader risk assessment.

 6

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

Five of six analog traces fall below the ensemble median bypass generation loss. This is expected:
the analog traces are selected to match the 24MS Most Probable scenario, which projects moderate
hydrological conditions for 2026. The full 30-trace ensemble, including higher-impact traces, is
retained for risk assessment and replacement power sizing.
This report was prepared by Argonne National Laboratory (Argonne) for the WAPA CRSP. Ar-
gonne developed TempRegPy, an internal Argonne dispatch optimization tool, and performed all
simulations and post-processing analyses described herein. WAPA provided the demand profiles,
operational constraints, and planning context that inform the analysis. The BoR operates GCD and
provided the Colorado River Mid-term Modeling System (CRMMS) hydrological data and tem-
perature input profiles used as boundary conditions. The analytical findings and interpretations in
this report are those of Argonne and do not necessarily represent the official positions of WAPA or
BoR.

1.3 Analysis Period
Four analysis periods are evaluated, defined by the combinations of two start dates (July 1 and
July 10, 2026) and two end dates (October 1 and October 20, 2026). These four periods bracket
the range of plausible implementation windows under consideration by BoR:
 1. July 1 – October 1 (93 days)
   2. July 1 – October 20 (112 days)
   3. July 10 – October 1 (84 days)
   4. July 10 – October 20 (103 days)
The July 1 start date corresponds to the earliest plausible operational start for temperature manage-
ment in 2026; July 10 reflects a later implementation scenario. The October 1 end date captures the
core bypass season; October 20 extends coverage through the period when SMB spawning activity
may still be ecologically significant. The October 20 cutoff reflects the biological assessment that
water temperatures after this date are unlikely to support sufficient SMB spawning because declin-
ing autumn temperatures either kill off juveniles or prevent growth sufficient to survive winter.
All cumulative metrics (total bypass volume, total generation, seasonal bypass generation loss, and
replacement power cost estimates) are computed independently for each of the four periods and
reported as co-equal analysis periods throughout this report.
The full simulation extends from June 1 through November 30, 2026, to capture the complete
bypass season from biological onset through cessation. Thermal characterization metrics (pen-
stock temperature profiles, thermal budget, bypass thermal effectiveness, downstream warming,
and compliance margin) are reported over this full simulation period because they describe physi-

 7

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

cal properties of the system that do not depend on the choice of analysis period. Bypass operations
typically begin in early-to-mid June when the 3-day rolling average of downstream temperature
first exceeds 15.5°C, with a median biological onset of June 9 across the 30-trace ensemble (range:
June 6 to June 18). All 30 traces require bypass operations beyond October 20, with a median
last-bypass date of November 17.
The daily cost table (Appendix F) provides day-by-day bypass generation loss at multiple per-
centile levels, enabling evaluation of any alternative start or end date by summing the relevant
rows. Approximately 24.2 GWh of bypass generation loss (median) occurs before July 1, and
38.3 GWh (median) occurs after October 20, representing bypass costs that fall outside the analy-
sis periods but are captured in the full simulation.

1.4 Report Organization
This report is organized into seven chapters. Chapter 2 establishes the analytical framework, in-
cluding the physical system model, key metric definitions, scenario design, and the dispatch opti-
mization formulation. Chapter 3 characterizes the thermal regime that drives bypass requirements,
covering penstock temperature profiles, downstream warming, the thermal budget, bypass effec-
tiveness, compliance margin, and unit cooling cost. Chapter 4 presents the bypass dispatch struc-
ture that emerges from the thermal constraints, including seasonal and diurnal patterns, monthly
distributions, and representative trace dispatch profiles. Chapter 5 quantifies the generation and
capacity impacts of RM15 compliance across the 30-trace ensemble. Chapter 6 translates these
impacts into a replacement power planning framework with percentile-level procurement tiers,
cost estimation, and monitoring triggers. Chapter 7 summarizes findings, planning implications,
limitations, and next steps. Supporting data, mathematical formulations, and supplementary fig-
ures are provided in the appendices.

Table 1.1 summarizes the key modeling assumptions used throughout this analysis.
The analytical framework used to evaluate these scenarios, including the physical system model,
key metric definitions, and the dispatch optimization formulation, is presented in the following
chapter.

Chapter 2

Analytical Framework and Scenario Design

2.1 Physical System and Temperature Mechanics
Glen Canyon Dam (GCD) on the Colorado River impounds Lake Powell and releases water through
two distinct pathways with different thermal characteristics:
 • Penstock (turbine outlet): Water passes through the power plant turbines, generating elec-
 tricity. The penstock release temperature Tpen (t) varies seasonally with reservoir stratifica-
 tion, typically ranging from approximately 9–17 ◦ C over the study period. Daily penstock
 temperatures are loaded from trace-specific input files and reflect the thermal structure of the
 reservoir under each hydrological scenario.
 • Bypass tubes (river outlet works): Water is released directly through the dam’s bypass
 system without passing through the turbines. The bypass release temperature Tbp (t) is gen-
 erally colder than the penstock temperature, varies by hydrological trace and day-of-year,
 and reflects the depth from which bypass water is drawn. This temperature differential is
 what makes bypass releases useful for downstream cooling.
The total release at GCD is the sum of the two flow components:

                                         qtot (t) = qtur (t) + qbp (t)                                 (2.1)

where qtot (t), qtur (t), and qbp (t) are the total, turbine, and bypass release flows at hour t, all in CFS.

Temperature mixing at the dam face. The outlet temperature at GCD is determined by an
energy balance of the mixed flows:

 qtur (t) · Tpen (t) + qbp (t) · Tbp (t)
                               Toutlet (t) =                                                           (2.2)
 qtot (t)

 10

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

When bypass water is colder than penstock water (Tbp < Tpen ), increasing the bypass fraction
reduces the mixed outlet temperature. The effectiveness of this cooling depends on the temperature
differential ∆Teff = Tpen − Tbp : a larger differential means each unit of bypass flow produces more
cooling, while a smaller differential requires proportionally more bypass volume to achieve the
same temperature reduction. Because both flow rates and temperatures are decision-dependent
quantities in the optimization, the numerator of Equation 2.2 contains bilinear product terms; these
are linearized using a sum-of-convex decomposition with N = 3 partitions (see Section A.3).

Back-calculation of required outlet temperature. To ensure that the downstream temperature
target is met after warming occurs, the outlet temperature at GCD must satisfy:

 Toutlet (t) ≤ Ttarget − ∆T (d, qtot ) (2.3)

where Ttarget = 15.5 ◦ C is the daily average temperature compliance threshold. This back-calculation
is the fundamental link between dam operations and downstream temperature compliance: the
larger the downstream warming ∆T , the colder the required outlet temperature, and the more by-
pass flow is needed.

RM15 compliance location. The RM15 compliance location and its implications for the thermal
budget are described in Section 1.1.

2.2 Bypass Generation Loss and Capacity Reduction Defini-
 tions
The fundamental cost of bypass operations is straightforward: water routed through the bypass
tubes cannot generate electricity. Every acre-foot diverted from the penstocks to the bypass system
represents foregone hydroelectric generation. This section defines the two complementary cost
metrics used throughout the report.

Bypass generation loss (GWh). The bypass generation loss quantifies the cumulative generation
that would have been produced if the bypass water had instead passed through the turbines. For a
given hour t:
 BypassGenLossMWh (t) = Qbp,AFH (t) × PCF(t) (2.4)

where Qbp,AFH (t) is the bypass flow in AFH and PCF(t) is the Power Conversion Factor in MW/AFH
for the corresponding day. Summing over all hours in the analysis period and dividing by 1,000

 11

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

yields the total bypass generation loss in GWh:

 1
 BypassGenLossGWh = Qbp,AFH (t) × PCF(t) (2.5)
 1,000 ∑
 t

Bypass capacity reduction (MW). The bypass capacity reduction quantifies the instantaneous
reduction in available generation during any given hour:

 BypassCapRedMW (t) = Qbp,AFH (t) × PCF(t) (2.6)

Note that the hourly computation is numerically identical to the bypass generation loss, the dif-
ference is in how the values are aggregated. Bypass generation loss is summed over time to yield
a cumulative GWh total; capacity reduction is examined as a distribution of hourly MW values,
characterizing when and how severely generation capacity is reduced. This distinction matters
operationally: a scenario with moderate total bypass generation loss but concentrated capacity
reduction during peak demand hours may be more consequential for WAPA’s firm capacity com-
mitments than a scenario with higher total bypass generation loss spread across off-peak hours.

Interpretation and approximation. The bypass generation loss assumes that bypassed water
would generate at the same efficiency (PCF) as the water actually passing through the turbines.
This is a reasonable approximation because the PCF depends primarily on net hydraulic head
(reservoir elevation minus tailwater elevation) and turbine efficiency, both of which are largely
independent of the turbine–bypass flow split at any given hour. The PCF conversion is exact within
the optimization model (R2 = 1.0); the < 2% approximation applies to the PCF factor relative to
the physical turbine performance curve. The full derivation and verification of the PCF identity are
presented in Section 2.3 and Figure A.2 (Appendix A).

Capacity reduction versus demand shortfall. Distinguish bypass capacity reduction from de-
mand shortfall. The capacity reduction defined above measures how much GCD’s generation is
reduced by bypass operations relative to what it could produce if all water went through the tur-
bines. A demand shortfall, by contrast, would measure the gap between WAPA’s customer demand
obligation and GCD’s actual generation. Because other CRSP generators can partially cover short-
falls, and because the Demand_MW values in the model represent a typical planning shape rather
than a firm contractual obligation, this report focuses on bypass capacity reduction as the primary
metric. The relationship between capacity reduction and potential replacement power needs is
discussed in Section 6.4.

Unit conversions. Flow and volume conversions used throughout the analysis:
 • CF_PER_AF = 43,560.02 ft3 /AF (cubic feet per acre-foot)

 12

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

 • CFS_TO_AFH = 3,600/CF_PER_AF ≈ 0.08264 AF/h per CFS (flow-to-volume conver-
 sion)

2.3 Power Conversion Factor
Turbine power output is related to turbine flow by a day-specific power conversion factor:

                      ptur (p,t) = qtur (p,t) · PCF(p, d) ∀ p ∈ P, t ∈ T (d)                   (2.7)

where PCF(p, d) is the power conversion factor in MW/CFS for plant p on day d, which depends
on net hydraulic head (reservoir pool elevation minus tailwater elevation) and turbine efficiency.
The PCF depends primarily on net hydraulic head, defined as the difference between the reservoir
pool elevation and the tailwater elevation, and secondarily on turbine efficiency characteristics.
Because pool elevation varies by trace and declines through the summer as releases draw down the
reservoir, the PCF varies both across traces and across months within each trace. Within the op-
timization model, the identity Ptur (t) = Qtur,AFH (t) × PCF(t) holds exactly (R2 = 1.0), confirming
that the PCF is the model’s internal conversion factor rather than an external approximation. The
verification of this relationship is shown in Figure A.2 (Appendix A).

2.4 Key Derived Metrics
Beyond the primary outputs of the optimization (flows, temperatures, generation), several derived
metrics are computed to characterize the thermal regime, the efficiency of bypass cooling, and the
cost of temperature compliance.

Thermal Budget (TB). The thermal budget quantifies how much “room” exists between the
compliance threshold and the combination of penstock temperature and downstream warming:

                            TB(d, qtot ) = Ttarget − ∆T (d, qtot ) − Tpen (d)                  (2.8)

where Ttarget = 15.5 ◦ C. When TB > 0, no bypass is needed. When TB < 0, bypass is required.

Bypass Thermal Effectiveness (∆Teff ). Measures the temperature differential that makes bypass
useful for cooling:
                               ∆Teff (d) = Tpen (d) − Tbp (d)                             (2.9)

Compliance Margin (CM). Measures how close the solution operates to the threshold:

                                CM(d) = Ttarget − TRM15,daily,wtd (d)                        (2.10)

 13

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

Cooling Effect (CE). Quantifies the temperature reduction achieved by bypass relative to a no-
bypass counterfactual:
                             CE(d) = Tcf (d) − TRM15,actual (d)                         (2.11)

Unit Cooling Cost (UCC). Expresses the cost of cooling in physical, energy, and capacity terms:

                          UCCAF (d) = Qbp,AF (d) / CE(d)                                    (2.12)
 UCCGWh (d) = BypassGenLossGWh (d) / CE(d) (2.13)
 UCCMW (d) = BypassCapRedMW (d) / CE(d) (2.14)

Marketable Capacity (EAC). The actual turbine power output at any hour:

                                         EAC(t) = Ptur (t)                                  (2.15)

Hourly Temperature Ramp. Captures the rate of temperature change at RM15:

                         ∆Tramp (t) = TRM15,hourly (t) − TRM15,hourly (t − 1)               (2.16)

2.5 Scenario Framework
The analysis evaluates 30 scenarios, one for each hydrological trace spanning the historical years
1991–2020 (labeled X1991 through X2020). The X prefix denotes a cross-applied historical year:
each trace applies the hydrological pattern of year YYYY to the 2026 reservoir and operational
context. Each trace represents the question: “What if 2026 hydrology resembles the conditions
observed in year X?” The 30-year historical record provides a range of hydrological conditions
spanning wet, dry, and average years, enabling probabilistic assessment of bypass requirements
and energy costs.

Trace-specific inputs. Each hydrological trace carries its own set of input data, reflecting the
unique thermal and hydrological conditions of the corresponding historical year:
 • Penstock temperature profile: daily turbine outlet temperatures, which vary by trace and
 day-of-year.
 • Bypass temperature profile: daily bypass inlet temperatures, reflecting the depth and ther-
 mal structure of the reservoir under each scenario.
 • Delta-T lookup table: RM15-specific downstream warming as a function of day of year
 and total flow (bilinear interpolation is used when inputs fall between table grid points).
 • Monthly release volumes: trace-specific total release volumes in kAF, loaded from the

 2.6 Analog Trace Selection
 Six analog traces. Six traces have been identified as the closest analogs to expected 2026 con-
 ditions under the May 2026 24-Month Study (24MS, Run ID 3317) Most Probable scenario [3]
 through a weighted Euclidean distance algorithm. The algorithm computes the normalized dis-
 tance between each historical trace and the 24MS target across three variables (monthly reservoir
 pool elevation, inflow volume, and release volume) for the June through November period. Each
 variable is normalized by the ensemble standard deviation to ensure commensurability, and weights
 of 0.5 (elevation), 0.3 (inflow), and 0.2 (release) are applied. Elevation receives the highest weight
 because it most directly determines penstock temperature and power conversion efficiency. Inflow
 captures the hydrological forcing that drives future elevation trajectories. Release receives the low-
 est weight because release schedules are largely prescribed by policy and vary less across traces

 15

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

than elevation or inflow. In priority order (closest first), the selected analogs are:
 1. X2011 – closest analog (distance = 228.95; October elevation 3,512.45 ft)
   2. X2015 (October elevation 3,512.83 ft)
   3. X2014 (October elevation 3,512.96 ft)
   4. X2007 (October elevation 3,513.05 ft)
   5. X2008 (October elevation 3,511.02 ft)
   6. X2001 (October elevation 3,510.92 ft)
The selection is robust to alternative weight specifications: all six traces are retained under elevation-
heavy weighting (0.7/0.2/0.1), and five of six are retained under equal weighting (0.33/0.33/0.33)
and inflow-heavy weighting (0.2/0.6/0.2). The full ranking and weight sensitivity analysis are
detailed in Appendix D.

Table 2.1: Characterization of the six Most Probable analog traces. Distance is the weighted Euclidean
distance to the May 2026 24MS Most Probable scenario. Ensemble rank is by bypass generation loss
(July 1–October 20, n = 30).

2.8 Dispatch Optimization Model
The scenarios defined in the preceding sections are each solved using a dispatch optimization that
optimizes GCD financial position subject to multiple operational constraints, of which the most
consequential is the daily flow-weighted average temperature at RM15 not exceeding 15.5◦ C. Ad-
ditional constraints include turbine and bypass flow bounds, hourly and daily ramp rate limits,
daily release volume targets, and rough zone avoidance (Appendix A). At each hour, the optimizer
decides how to allocate the total scheduled release between turbine flow, which passes through
the penstocks and generates power, and bypass flow, which exits through the river outlet works,
produces no power, but delivers colder water that reduces the mixed outlet temperature. The math-
ematical model is formulated as a Mixed-Integer Linear Program (MILP) using Google OR-Tools
MathOpt [7] and solved using the HiGHS open-source solver [9]. To keep the problem tractable
over a multi-month season, the study period is decomposed into consecutive rolling weekly win-
dows of 168 hours each; each window is solved independently with an 8-minute time limit and
a 3% relative optimality gap tolerance. In practice, the observed optimality gap is typically well
below 1% for most weekly windows; the 3% tolerance serves as a conservative upper bound to en-
sure solver termination within the time limit. The complete mathematical formulation, including
the bilinear product linearization for the temperature mixing equation, is provided in Appendix A.
The analysis evaluates 30 hydrological traces, each solved independently. Every trace carries its
own set of trace-specific inputs: daily penstock temperatures (reflecting the reservoir’s thermal
stratification under that hydrological year), daily bypass inlet temperatures, monthly release vol-
umes, and end-of-month reservoir elevations from the Colorado River Mid-term Modeling System
(CRMMS). Temperature mixing at the dam face follows an energy balance between the penstock
and bypass flows; downstream warming from GCD to RM15 is captured through a flow- and
Julian-day-dependent lookup table, so that higher total releases reduce travel time and attenuate
warming. The Power Conversion Factor (PCF) is computed from net hydraulic head and varies
by trace and month as reservoir elevation changes. All 30 scenarios solved successfully with no
infeasibilities.

2.8.1 Flow Rate Limits
Table 2.2 presents the configured maximum turbine and bypass flow rates by month. The bypass
capacity is fixed at 15,000 CFS throughout the study period, while the maximum turbine flow
varies by month, reflecting planned maintenance and refurbishment schedules for the generating
units. These limits are scenario-independent and apply uniformly across all traces. The resulting

 19

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

unconstrained generation capacity, the maximum power output achievable if all water were routed
through the turbines, varies by month due to both the turbine flow limits and the trace-dependent
PCF.

and resulting unconstrained capacity. Turbine flow limits reflect planned maintenance schedules.

2.9 Data Pipeline and Reproducibility
The analysis draws on four primary data sources. Monthly release volumes and end-of-month
reservoir elevations are obtained from the Colorado River Mid-term Modeling System (CRMMS)
database maintained by the Bureau of Reclamation (BoR) [4]. Hourly price projections are gen-
erated by a forward-price-based projection model that adjusts current Argus forward curves [2]
with the distribution of historical price premiums. Demand profiles are derived from WAPA’s
average monthly load profiles, which specify the typical weekly demand shape for each month.
Trace-specific temperature profiles (daily penstock and bypass inlet temperatures) and the down-
stream warming lookup table are loaded from input files prepared by BoR separately for each of
the 30 hydrological scenarios.
The analytical pipeline proceeds in five stages. First, the multi-variable distance algorithm incor-
porating monthly elevation, inflow, and release data (Appendix D) selects the six analog traces by
comparing each trace’s monthly elevations, inflows, and releases against the May 2026 24-Month
Study (Run ID 3317) most probable inflow scenario. Second, daily release targets and PCF val-
ues are computed from the CRMMS elevation and release data. Third, the TempRegPy MILP
optimization is executed for each of the 30 traces using rolling weekly windows. Fourth, results
are aggregated to daily, monthly, and seasonal summaries. Fifth, percentile computations, figure
generation, and table population are performed using postprocessing scripts.
A separate README file maintained alongside the code repository provides step-by-step instruc-
tions for reproducing the analysis from raw CRMMS inputs through final report compilation. The

 20

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

LaTeX document is compiled with populated tables and figures generated by the postprocessing
pipeline, ensuring that all reported values trace directly to the optimization output files.
With the analytical framework and scenario design established, the following chapter examines the
thermal regime that drives bypass requirements across the 30-trace ensemble.

 21

Chapter 3

Thermal Regime and Bypass Requirements

This chapter establishes the thermal context that drives bypass operations. The causal chain is
straightforward: penstock temperature, the dominant driver of bypass requirements, rises through
summer, consuming the thermal budget and triggering bypass operations whose efficiency depends
on the penstock–bypass temperature differential. The seasonal dispatch patterns that result from
these thermal drivers are presented in Chapter 4.

3.1 Penstock Temperature Profiles
Penstock temperature is the single most important driver of bypass requirements. Across the 30-
trace ensemble, penstock temperatures follow a characteristic seasonal arc: rising through July and
August as the thermocline deepens, peaking in late August or September, and declining through
October. The magnitude and timing of this peak vary across traces because each hydrological
scenario produces a different reservoir elevation and stratification pattern; traces with higher pool
elevations draw from deeper, cooler water, producing lower penstock temperatures and reduced
bypass requirements.
Figure 3.1 presents the daily penstock temperature profiles across the ensemble, with the six analog
traces overlaid. When penstock temperature approaches or exceeds the 15.5◦ C threshold, even
modest downstream warming makes compliance impossible without bypass.
The rate at which penstock temperature rises through early summer determines bypass onset; the
peak temperature, combined with concurrent downstream warming, determines maximum bypass
intensity; and the autumn decline determines cessation.

 3.2 Downstream Warming
 Downstream warming ∆T from GCD to RM15 depends on two factors: (1) day of year, which
 governs solar radiation and air temperature (∆T peaks in late June through July and declines
 through autumn); and (2) total release flow rate, where higher flows reduce transit time and warm-
 ing. This flow dependence creates an important feedback: bypass increases total release, which
 reduces ∆T , providing a secondary cooling benefit beyond the direct mixing effect at the dam face.
 The optimizer captures this feedback through the flow-dependent ∆T lookup table (Appendix A,
   Figure 3.2).
 Figure 3.3 shows ∆T across the ensemble. Because RM15 is only 30 miles downstream, ∆T is
 moderate relative to more distant compliance locations, making penstock temperature the domi-
 nant thermal budget driver. The narrow inter-trace spread before October reflects nearly identical
 release schedules during June–September (Table B.4); in October, releases diverge across traces,
 producing visible spread in ∆T .

 3.3 Thermal Budget Analysis
 Each trace’s daily thermal budget is evaluated at the realized total release flow for that trace-day.
 When T B > 0, no bypass is needed; when T B < 0, bypass is required, with the magnitude indicat-
 ing thermal stress severity (e.g., T B = −5◦ C requires substantially more bypass than T B = −1◦ C).
 The thermal budget is a first-order diagnostic: it does not account for the nonlinear blending dy-
 namics of the mixing equation (Equation 2.2), but the correlation with actual bypass fraction is
 strong (ρ = −0.989; Figure E.11, Appendix F).

 Figure 3.4 presents the daily thermal budget across the ensemble. The seasonal pattern mirrors
 the penstock temperature profile: T B turns negative as penstock temperatures rise through July,
 reaches its most negative values during peak summer, and recovers in October. The zero-crossing
 dates correspond approximately to bypass onset and cessation, though the exact schedule also
 depends on flow constraints, demand patterns, and bypass thermal effectiveness. The relationship
 between thermal budget and bypass intensity is examined in Figure E.11 (Appendix F).

 scenarios that are not visible in the ensemble fan chart, where individual trace trajectories are
 obscured by the percentile bands.

 3.4 Bypass Thermal Effectiveness
 The bypass thermal effectiveness ∆Teff (d) = Tpen (d) − Tbp (d) (Equation 2.9) determines how ef-
 ficiently each unit of bypass flow translates into downstream temperature reduction. When ∆Teff
 is large (exceeding 10◦ C), modest bypass fractions achieve compliance; when it is small (below
 6◦ C), much larger volumes are needed for the same temperature reduction. In the limiting case
 where ∆Teff approaches zero, bypass becomes ineffective regardless of volume.
 Effectiveness varies seasonally and across traces: it tends to be large early in summer when the
 reservoir is strongly stratified, and narrows as the thermocline deepens through the season. Inter-
 trace variability reflects differences in reservoir stratification across the 30 hydrological scenarios.

 deficit but also on bypass efficiency: a trace with moderate deficit but high effectiveness may incur
 less bypass generation loss than one with a smaller deficit but low effectiveness.

 the distribution of hourly temperature ramps at the dam face and at RM15, Figure E.9 presents

 3.5 Compliance Margin
 Under the financially optimal dispatch, 76.9% of active bypass-days operate within 0.5 ◦ C of the
 15.5 ◦ C threshold, with a mean compliance margin of 0.63 ◦ C (Appendix E.1, Figures E.1 and E.2).
 This near-threshold operation confirms that the optimizer produces the minimum bypass volume
 consistent with compliance.

 3.6 Cooling Benefit and Diminishing Returns
 The cumulative cooling benefit relative to a no-bypass counterfactual (Figure 3.8) is a median
 of 781.2 degree-days across the ensemble, achieved over 130 days with meaningful cooling, at
 a median cost of 0.438 GWh per degree-day. The bypass-volume-to-cooling-effect relationship
 is convex (Figure 3.9): marginal compliance cost increases as the thermal budget deficit grows,
 so small reductions in the threshold could produce disproportionately large increases in bypass
 generation loss.

 3.7 Unit Cooling Cost
 Three Unit Cooling Cost metrics normalize bypass cost by the cooling effect achieved: UCCAF
 (AF/◦ C) measures bypass volume per degree of cooling; UCCGWh (GWh/◦ C) measures bypass
 generation loss per degree of cooling, the metric most relevant to replacement power cost (Sec-
 tion 6.6); and UCCMW (MW/◦ C) measures average capacity reduction per degree of cooling. All
 three are computed only for days with bypass fraction exceeding 5% and cooling effect exceeding
 0.5◦ C.
 Unit cooling cost varies systematically through the season, driven by ∆Teff : low early when the
 penstock–bypass differential is large, rising during peak summer as the differential narrows, and
 declining in autumn.
 Figure 3.10 presents UCCGWh , which incorporates the PCF and thus reflects both thermal and
 hydraulic dimensions of bypass generation loss. The analog traces are highlighted to indicate the
 expected cost regime for summer 2026.
 The capacity-basis unit cooling cost by demand tier is provided in Appendix F: Figure E.5 shows
 the peak-hour UCCMW , Figure E.6 the mid-peak-hour UCCMW , and Figure E.7 the off-peak
 UCCMW . The thermal drivers and cost-effectiveness metrics established in this chapter determine

classified into one of three demand tiers based on WAPA’s monthly demand shape:
 • Peak (8 hours): the 8 hours per day with the highest demand
 • Mid (8 hours): the 8 hours with intermediate demand
 • Off-Peak (8 hours): the 8 hours with the lowest demand

 4.2 Seasonal Bypass Patterns
 Bypass operations follow the seasonal arc of penstock temperatures established in Section 3.1:
 bypass requirements emerge in mid-June, intensify through July as penstock temperatures climb,
 reach peak levels during late July through early September when the thermal budget deficit is most
 severe, and diminish through October. During the peak period, the median daily bypass fraction
 exceeds 50% of total release, and the optimizer allocates the maximum feasible bypass volume
 during off-peak hours to preserve peak-hour generation capacity.

 seasonal rise in penstock temperature (Section 3.1). The inter-trace spread is narrowest in August
 and September (coefficient of variation approximately 4–5%), when nearly all traces require heavy
 bypass. July exhibits a wider distribution because onset timing varies across traces, and October is
 broader still, reflecting the divergence in October release volumes (Table B.4).
 The practical implication is that August and September bypass volumes are highly predictable,
 enabling confident pre-season procurement, while July and October volumes carry greater uncer-
 tainty and suggest conditional procurement as the season unfolds. The monthly bypass statistics
 are tabulated in Table 4.3.

 36

 Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

 4.4 Bypass Flow Characteristics
 This section quantifies the relationship between penstock temperature and bypass intensity and
 presents the ensemble-wide bypass flow statistics that characterize the operational regime under
 RM15 compliance.
 Figure 4.4 presents bypass fraction versus penstock temperature across all trace-days. The rela-
 tionship is strongly nonlinear: bypass fraction is near zero below approximately 14◦ C, rises steeply
 through the 14–18◦ C transition zone, and saturates at 55–65% above 20◦ C. Saturation reflects the
 15,000 CFS bypass tube capacity constraint: once penstock temperatures are sufficiently high,
 further warming cannot be fully offset. The scatter around the central trend reflects secondary fac-
 tors including bypass thermal effectiveness, downstream warming magnitude, and demand-driven
 dispatch timing.
 The general S-curve shape of this relationship is a stable physical property arising from the mixing
 equation and bypass capacity constraint; however, the specific transition temperatures and satura-
 tion levels would shift year-to-year with changes in reservoir elevation, bypass inlet temperature,
 downstream warming characteristics, and compliance location.

 4.5 Diurnal Dispatch Structure
 The dispatch optimization concentrates bypass in hours of lowest demand value and preserves
 turbine generation during peak demand hours, producing a characteristic diurnal dispatch structure
 that varies by month and day type. This structure determines the temporal distribution of capacity
 reduction across demand tiers and reveals the degree to which the optimizer can protect peak-hour
 generation despite the large bypass volumes required for compliance.

 Figure 4.6: Hourly bypass frequency heatmap with median available capacity (MW).

 Figure 4.5 presents the hourly bypass frequency heatmap with median capacity reduction anno-
 tations. During peak summer weeks, bypass is active in nearly 100% of traces during off-peak
 and mid-tier hours (capacity reductions of 140–160 MW), while peak demand hours show lower
 bypass intensity (80–120 MW), reflecting the optimizer’s strategy of preserving high-value gen-
 eration. The transition between regimes is gradual, consistent with the ramp penalty structure
 (Appendix A). During shoulder weeks, bypass is concentrated in fewer hours and fewer traces.
 The complementary generation heatmap (Figure 4.6) illustrates how the optimizer shapes the gen-
 eration profile to match the demand curve as closely as the temperature constraint permits.

4.6 Representative Trace Dispatch
The ensemble-level statistics and median dispatch profiles presented in the preceding subsections
characterize the central tendency and spread of bypass operations, but they do not reveal the hour-
by-hour operational reality that WAPA schedulers would face during the most thermally stressed
period of the season. This subsection presents detailed dispatch profiles for the peak-bypass week
of each analog trace, providing a concrete illustration of how the optimizer allocates flow between
turbines and bypass under the most demanding conditions.
Figure 4.13 and 4.13 present the peak-bypass week dispatch for each of the six analog traces.
It shows hourly turbine and bypass flow, with the corresponding outlet and RM15 temperature
overlaid. The peak-bypass week is defined as the seven consecutive days with the highest total
bypass volume for each trace; this typically falls in late August or early September, which coincides
with the seasonal maximum in penstock temperature as shown earlier.
Several features of the peak-week dispatch are consistent across the analog traces. First, the diur-
nal bypass pattern described in earlier is clearly visible at the individual-trace level: bypass flow is
highest during off-peak hours (typically 2,500–4,000 CFS) and lowest during peak demand hours
(typically 1,000–2,500 CFS), with gradual transitions between the two regimes. Second, the tur-
bine flow profile mirrors this pattern inversely: turbine flow peaks during high-demand hours and
reaches its minimum during off-peak hours, with the minimum often approaching the configured
turbine floor. Third, the temperature overlay confirms that the daily flow-weighted average RM15
temperature remains at or just below the 15.5◦ C threshold on every day of the peak week, which is
consistent with the near-threshold compliance margin reported in Section 3.5. The hourly RM15
temperature shows modest diurnal variation (typically 0.3–0.8◦ C peak-to-trough) and is driven by
the changing bypass fraction through the day; this variation is attenuated relative to the dam-face
temperature variation because the 15-mile reach provides natural thermal buffering.
Differences among the analog traces during their respective peak weeks reflect the underlying
thermal conditions. X2015, the most favorable analog, shows lower bypass fractions and higher
turbine flows during peak hours than X2007, the least favorable analog, because X2015’s lower
penstock temperature provides a larger thermal budget and requires less bypass to maintain com-
pliance. The spread in peak-week bypass intensity across the six analogs is consistent with the
spread in season-total bypass generation loss reported in Table 2.1. This confirms that the peak
week is representative of the broader seasonal differences among traces.
These trace-level dispatch profiles complement the ensemble-level views in Figures 4.5 and 4.7
by providing the operational detail needed for scheduling decisions. The flow allocations shown
in Figure 4.13 represent the type of dispatch schedule that WAPA would implement during the
most thermally constrained period of the bypass season, and illustrate the practical limits of the
optimizer’s ability to protect peak-hour generation when the thermal budget deficit is most severe.

 4.7 Release Composition Through the Season
 The release composition, defined as the monthly split between turbine and bypass water, is the fun-
 damental quantity linking the thermal regime to generation and capacity impacts: every acre-foot
 routed through bypass rather than the penstocks represents foregone generation, and the monthly
 split determines both the seasonal energy shortfall and the capacity reduction that WAPA must plan
 around.
 Figure 4.14 presents the monthly release composition across the ensemble. Total monthly release
 is nearly identical across traces for July through September (Table 4.3), because the operational
 schedule prescribes uniform volumes. The turbine share declines steadily as bypass requirements
 intensify: median turbine releases fall from 272 kAF in July (56%) to 193 kAF in September
 (41%), while bypass releases rise from 210 kAF to 273 kAF, reflecting the seasonal penstock
 temperature rise (Section 3.1).

 of bypass fraction is narrow during July through September (3–4 percentage points), confirming
 that bypass requirements vary only modestly across hydrological scenarios during peak summer.

643,000 AF; Table B.4) and greater variability in bypass cessation timing. The season-total me-
dian bypass fraction is 52.2%, though this understates peak-month intensity: September reaches
58.6%, reflecting the combination of peak penstock temperatures and reduced turbine flow limits
(Table 2.2).
The practical consequence for planning is that July through September replacement power can
treat total release as effectively deterministic and focus on the bypass fraction alone, whereas
October planning must account for uncertainty in both the bypass fraction and the total release
volume. These dispatch patterns directly determine the generation and capacity impacts quantified
in Chapter 5.

 51

Chapter 5

Generation and Capacity Impacts

This chapter translates the thermal regime established in Chapter 3 into generation and capacity
impacts. Two complementary dimensions are reported: energy (cumulative GWh) and capacity
(instantaneous MW). Both matter for different reasons — energy for seasonal accounting; capacity
for real-time dispatch and firm commitment obligations. A scenario that loses less total energy but
concentrates that loss during peak demand hours may be more operationally consequential than
one with higher total loss spread across off-peak periods. All results are presented as distributions
across the 30-trace ensemble, with the six analog traces highlighted for planning context.
All generation and capacity metrics are reported for the four co-equal analysis periods defined
in Section 1.3. Under RM15 compliance, median bypass generation loss ranges from 245.9 to
319.1 GWh depending on the analysis period (Table 2), representing the direct energy cost of
temperature compliance. In capacity terms, bypass operations reduce peak-hour generation by a
median of 88 to 99 MW averaged over the season, with the most severe reductions in August and
September.

5.1 Total Generation Under RM15 Compliance
5.1.1 Total Generation Summary
Total generation under RM15 compliance ranges from 429.2 GWh (P10) to 474.4 GWh (P90)
across the 30-trace ensemble, with a median of 448.8 GWh (Table 5.1; this window includes
pre-July 1 bypass). The interquartile range of approximately 29 GWh (6% of median) indicates
that hydrological uncertainty produces modest variation in total generation outcomes. At the me-
dian, approximately 43% of unconstrained generation potential is consumed by bypass operations.
Across the four co-equal analysis periods, median bypass generation loss ranges from 245.9 to
319.1 GWh (Table 2), with P10–P90 spreads of approximately 20–40 GWh depending on the win-

5.1.3 Trace Impact Ranking
Figure 5.3 ranks all 30 traces by total bypass generation loss. The highest-impact traces are charac-
terized by the highest season-average penstock temperatures and, secondarily, by the highest PCF
values, confirming the driver hierarchy established in Chapter 3. Among the analogs, X2015 is
positioned toward the favorable end and X2007 toward the constrained end. X1995 is a notable
outlier, reflecting the exceptionally wet 1995 water year.
The coefficient of variation differs substantially between energy and capacity impacts: bypass
generation loss has a CV of approximately 5%, while peak-tier capacity reduction has a CV of
approximately 18% (3.3× more uncertain). This asymmetry has direct procurement implications:
energy replacement can be sized with high confidence at the P10 level, while capacity replacement

5.2 Bypass Generation Loss Decomposition
The unconstrained generation potential decomposes into two components: bypass generation loss
(the energy foregone by routing water through the bypass tubes) and actual generation (the energy
produced through the penstocks under RM15 compliance). For the simulation-start through Oc-
tober 20 window, bypass generation loss is projected to account for approximately 342 GWh at
the median out of a total unconstrained potential of approximately 791 GWh, meaning that RM15
compliance is projected to consume roughly 43% of the generation that GCD could otherwise pro-
duce with the same release schedule. Across the four co-equal analysis periods, the median bypass
generation loss ranges from 245.9 to 319.1 GWh depending on the analysis period (Table 2). The
decomposition thereby quantifies the direct energy cost of temperature compliance in a form that
maps cleanly onto the replacement power framework.
The bypass generation loss is not distributed uniformly across the season. August and September
are projected to account for the largest share of bypass generation loss because penstock temper-
atures peak during these months (Section 3.1), driving the thermal budget to its most negative
values and requiring the highest bypass fractions to maintain compliance at RM15. The monthly
disaggregation of generation by demand tier, presented in the following section, reveals how the
optimizer distributes the remaining generation capacity across peak, mid, and off-peak hours in
each month.

5.3 Power Conversion Analysis
An important interaction exists between reservoir elevation and bypass generation loss. When pool
elevation is high, the Power Conversion Factor (PCF) is also high, meaning each acre-foot of water
diverted to bypass represents more foregone generation. However, higher pool elevations are also
associated with cooler penstock temperatures, which reduce the total volume of bypass required
for temperature compliance. Across the 30-trace ensemble, the volume reduction effect dominates:
traces with higher PCF require substantially less bypass, producing lower total bypass generation
loss despite the higher per-unit cost. Figure 5.4 illustrates this net relationship.
Across the 30-trace ensemble, the Spearman correlation between season-average penstock temper-
ature and total bypass generation loss is ρ = 0.79 (p < 0.0001), confirming that penstock tempera-
ture, the dominant driver of bypass requirements (Section 3.1), provides a basis for elevation-based
trace selection.

5.4 Generation by Demand Tier
The dispatch optimization preserves peak-tier generation at the expense of mid-tier and off-peak

reduction distributions form the basis for the replacement power framework.

 Figure 5.12: Extended bypass horizon showing the bypass active period for each trace. The vertical line
 marks the October 20 reporting cutoff. Bar segments distinguish bypass generation loss within the primary

 All 30 traces require bypass beyond October 20, with last-bypass dates ranging from November 15

 32.9 GWh (P10) to 50.7 GWh (P90), with a median of 38.3 GWh. These values are not included
 in the primary ensemble statistics, so reported season-total figures systematically understate the
 full cost of RM15 compliance. Trace-by-trace details are provided in Table 5.10. The October 20
 cutoff reflects the biological assessment that post-October temperatures are unlikely to support
 sufficient smallmouth bass spawning to threaten native fish populations.

Analysis of the 3-day rolling average temperature threshold (15.5◦ C) reveals that bypass operations
are triggered between June 6 and June 18 across the ensemble, with a median onset of June 9. All
30 traces require continuous bypass through at least November 17. The median gap between
biological onset and the July 1 reporting start is 22 days, during which a median of 24.2 GWh of
bypass generation loss occurs (P10: 20.0, P90: 29.6 GWh). This pre-July 1 bypass cost represents
approximately 6.3% of the full-season bypass generation loss and quantifies the generation impact
that would be incurred if temperature management operations began at the biological trigger date
rather than the July 1 reporting start.

 76

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

The bypass season boundaries are not fixed calendar dates, they depend on when penstock temper-
atures cross the thermal budget threshold, which varies across the 30 hydrological traces. For most
traces, the theoretical onset of bypass requirements occurs in early-to-mid June, and the last date
bypass is needed extends into mid-November. However, WAPA and the Bureau of Reclamation
(BoR) have considered alternative implementation windows that differ from the full theoretical
bypass season, either by starting later in summer or ending earlier in autumn.

 77

Chapter 6

Replacement Power Planning and Capacity
Assessment

This chapter translates the generation and capacity impacts from Chapter 5 into a replacement
power planning and capacity assessment framework. All metrics are reported for the four co-equal
analysis periods defined in Section 1.3.
The analytical logic proceeds in five steps: i) Present a percentile-level replacement power frame-
work that sizes replacement capacity and energy purchases (Section 6.1); ii) Characterize the ca-
pacity and energy shortfall that bypass operations create (Section 6.2); iii) Establish what GCD can
reliably deliver under RM15 compliance at various percentile levels (Section 6.3); iv) Estimate the
replacement power cost of bypass generation loss across hydrological and price scenarios (Sec-
tion 6.6); v) Specify monitoring triggers and scheduling strategies for adaptive replacement power
planning (Sections 6.7 and 6.8).
This chapter is descriptive, not prescriptive: it provides the analytical framework for WAPA’s
replacement power decisions without presuming WAPA’s risk tolerance or Deliverable Service
Amount (DSA) obligations.

6.1 Replacement Power Percentile-Level Framework
The replacement power framework is organized around five percentile levels (P10, P25, P50, P75,
and P90) drawn from the 30-trace ensemble.1 Each percentile level represents a different balance
between the risk of under-procurement and the cost of over-procurement.
 1 Throughout this report, PX denotes the Xth percentile of the 30-trace ensemble distribution.P10 = the value
exceeded by 90% of traces (low-impact end); P90 = the value exceeded by only 10% of traces (high-impact end). A
replacement power acquisition sized at PX would be sufficient for approximately X% of ensemble outcomes.

 78

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

The choice of percentile level is a planning decision that depends on factors outside the scope of
this analysis, including the contractual structure of the DSA, the consequences of under-delivery,
the availability of other CRSP resources, and the degree of real-time operational flexibility avail-
able to WAPA. Feedback received during the review of preliminary results indicated that the P10,
P25, and P50 levels may be the most relevant for replacement power sizing under current CRSP
planning practices, though the full percentile distribution is reported throughout this chapter for
completeness.
The framework applies to both the capacity (MW) and energy (GWh) dimensions of the bypass
shortfall. In the capacity dimension, each percentile level specifies the average bypass capacity
reduction during a given month and demand tier; the incremental difference between adjacent
percentile levels indicates the additional replacement capacity required to move from one confi-
dence level to the next. In the energy dimension, the shortfall for a given month and demand tier
equals the capacity reduction multiplied by the number of hours in that tier during the month.
Across the four co-equal analysis periods, the total bypass generation loss ranges from 235.9GWh
(P10, July10–October1) to 337.5GWh (P90, July1–October20), with a P10-to-P90 span of approx-
imately 20–40GWh depending on the window (Table2). This relatively narrow spread, correspond-
ing to a coefficient of variation of approximately 5%, confirms that the energy shortfall is highly
predictable once the analysis period is fixed.
Inverting the framework yields a complementary characterization of what GCD can reliably de-
liver under RM15 compliance. The marketable capacity at each percentile level equals the un-
constrained monthly capacity minus the capacity reduction at that percentile. This inversion is
presented in Section 6.3.
One possible interpretation is a tiered acquisition strategy: a base tranche secured pre-season at
forward prices, an intermediate increment acquired as early-summer conditions clarify, and a tail
increment reserved for real-time acquisition if observed penstock temperatures confirm a high-
impact trajectory. The incremental tranches sum to the chosen percentile level by construction.
This tiered approach is presented as one possible translation of the framework, not as a recom-
mendation. The monitoring triggers (Section 6.7) provide the observational basis for updating the
applicable percentile level as the season unfolds.

report the average bypass capacity reduction in MW, and the energy columns convert these values
into cumulative GWh by multiplying by the number of hours in each tier-month. The incremental
columns (∆P50 → P75 and ∆P75 → P90) indicate the additional replacement capacity required to

Note: Capacity values = average bypass capacity reduction during the specified demand tier hours, with percentiles
taken across the 30-trace ensemble. Energy values = total bypass generation loss during the specified tier hours for
the month (converted to GWh). ∆ columns show incremental replacement power required to move between percentile
levels. The applicable percentile level depends on the relevant risk tolerance and DSA obligations.

Several features of the shortfall distribution merit attention. First, the incremental capacity be-
tween adjacent percentile levels is small relative to the base P50 value for most months and tiers.
During August peak hours, moving from P50 to P75 coverage requires an additional 8.1MW, and
moving from P75 to P90 requires a further 3.1MW, compared to a P50 base of 110.6 MW. This
concentration indicates that the marginal cost of moving from median to high-confidence coverage
is modest in capacity terms.
Second, the shortfall is not distributed uniformly across demand tiers. The dispatch optimiza-
tion concentrates bypass in off-peak hours to preserve peak-hour generation (Section4.5), so the
off-peak capacity reduction is systematically larger than the peak-tier reduction in every month.
During August, the P50 off-peak capacity reduction is 143.0MW compared to 110.6MW during
peak hours, a difference of 32.4MW that reflects the optimizer’s strategy of protecting high-value
generation.
Third, the inter-tier spread widens in October relative to the mid-summer months. The ∆P75 →
P90 for October mid-tier capacity is 38.3MW, compared to 2.7MW for August mid-tier. This
widening reflects the divergence in October release volumes across traces (Table B.4) and the

 80

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

greater variability in bypass cessation timing as penstock temperatures decline at different rates
across the ensemble.
The shortfall values in Table 6.1 provide the direct input for the block purchase sizing strategies
presented in Section 6.4. Each block purchase strategy translates these month-by-tier shortfall val-
ues into a specific procurement structure with defined capacity, energy, cost, and residual exposure.

6.3 Marketable Capacity Under RM15 Compliance
Table 6.2 inverts the shortfall characterization to report what GCD can reliably deliver under RM15
compliance. Two baselines are provided: the theoretical unconstrained capacity (maximum turbine
flow, no bypass) and the practical unconstrained capacity (P95 of observed generation during low-
bypass hours, reflecting normal operational constraints without the temperature constraint). The
practical baseline is substantially lower because daily release targets, ramp limits, and rough zone
avoidance prevent sustained maximum-flow operation. Marketable capacity equals the applicable
baseline minus the bypass capacity reduction at each percentile. During August peak hours, P90
marketable capacity is 204.1 MW (practical) or 547.7 MW (theoretical). September practical
capacity is unavailable due to insufficient low-bypass hours.

the P95 of observed generation during low-bypass hours (<5% bypass fraction). Marketable capacity =
unconstrained capacity minus bypass capacity reduction at the specified percentile level. Both baselines are
provided for WAPA’s planning discretion.

The relationship between penstock temperature and bypass intensity (Figure 4.4, Section 4.4)
shows the nonlinear transition from low bypass at penstock temperatures below approximately
14◦ C to saturated bypass above 20◦ C, consistent with the thermal budget analysis in Section 3.3.
This relationship provides the physical basis for the monitoring triggers described in Section 6.7:

as observed penstock temperatures are compared against the ensemble in real-time, the applicable
percentile level for marketable capacity can be updated.

6.4 Block Purchase Sizing Strategies
This section presents three procurement strategies that translate the shortfall distributions from
Section 6.2 into actionable block purchase structures. The strategies differ in temporal granularity,
operational complexity, and the fraction of total bypass generation loss they cover:
 1. Monthly shaped block purchases (Section6.4.1): Separate blocks sized for each month,
 allowing the procurement to track the seasonal evolution of bypass intensity. This strategy
 provides the highest coverage but requires monthly re-contracting.
 2. Season-spanning block purchase (Section6.4.2): A single set of nested blocks sized to
 cover the entire July–October period, providing a conservative pre-season commitment that
 can be supplemented with spot market purchases.
 3. Single-strategy held block (Section 6.4.3): A block sized on July conditions and held un-
 changed across the full season, representing the simplest possible procurement structure.
Each strategy is evaluated at five percentile levels (P10 through P90) and under three price scenar-
ios (P10, P50, and P90 wholesale prices). The block purchase tables present two complementary
decompositions of the bypass shortfall. The demand-tier decomposition (Off-peak, Mid, Peak
rows) shows the shortfall by time-of-day category, indicating how much replacement power is
needed during each 8-hour tier. The nested-tier decomposition (Tier1, Tier2, Tier3 rows) shows
the shortfall as a set of stacked blocks of decreasing duration: Tier1 is a 24-hour flat block sized at
the minimum hourly shortfall across all tiers, Tier2 adds incremental capacity for the 16 Mid and
Off-Peak hours, and Tier3 adds a further increment for the 8 Off-Peak hours only. The demand-tier
view answers the question how much capacity is lost during peak hours versus off-peak hours?”
while the nested-tier view answers the question what combination of flat and shaped blocks covers
the full shortfall?”
The Energy Exposure column in each table reports the fraction of total monthly bypass generation
loss that is not covered by the specified block structure. An Energy Exposure of 0% means the
block fully covers the shortfall in that tier; an exposure of 50% means half the monthly bypass
generation loss remains uncovered and must be cleared through spot market purchases or other
flexible resources. Energy Exposure is computed hour by hour, so surplus in one hour cannot
offset a shortfall in another.

 82

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

6.4.1 Monthly Shaped Block Purchases
The monthly shaped block strategy sizes separate replacement power blocks for each month, allow-
ing procurement to track the seasonal arc of bypass intensity. This provides the highest coverage
fraction but requires separate monthly transactions.
Tables6.3 through6.6 present the block purchase sizing options for each month at five percentile
levels. August has the greatest bypass intensity (P50 peak-tier reduction of 110.6MW, total loss of
93.9GWh), while September has the highest peak-tier capacity reduction (119.2MW at P50) due
to the combination of peak penstock temperatures and reduced turbine flow limits. Table6.7 and
Figure 6.1 summarize the peak-tier structure across all months.
The monthly shaped block strategy sizes separate replacement power blocks for each month of
the bypass season. Because bypass intensity varies substantially across the season (Section 4.2),
monthly sizing allows the procurement to track the seasonal arc: lower blocks in July when bypass
requirements are ramping up, larger blocks in August and September when penstock temperatures
peak, and reduced blocks in October as temperatures decline. This strategy provides the highest
coverage fraction of the three approaches but requires WAPA to execute separate procurement
transactions for each month.
Tables6.3 through 6.6 present the block purchase sizing options for July, August, September, and
October 2026, respectively. Each table reports the capacity (MW), energy (GWh), Energy Expo-
sure (%), and estimated cost ($M) at five percentile levels (P10, P25, P50, P75, P90) for both the
demand-tier and nested-tier decompositions. August is the month of greatest bypass intensity: at
the P50 level, the peak-tier capacity reduction is 110.6MW and the total monthly bypass generation
loss is 93.9 GWh (Table 6.4). September has the highest peak-tier capacity reduction (119.2 MW
at P50) because the combination of peak penstock temperatures and reduced turbine flow limits
constrains output most severely.
Table6.7 summarizes the peak-tier capacity reduction and energy loss across all months at each
percentile level, providing a compact reference for sizing peak-hour replacement power. Figure6.1
presents the same information graphically as stacked bars showing the tiered replacement power
structure by month. The firm base (P10) represents capacity reduction that occurs in more than
90% of ensemble scenarios; successive tiers add capacity at decreasing confidence of need.

6.4.2 Season-Spanning Block Purchase
The season-spanning strategy sizes a single set of nested blocks to cover the entire July–October
period, providing a conservative pre-season commitment. Rather than sizing separate blocks for
each month, this strategy identifies the minimum capacity reduction that persists across all hours
in the window, then adds incremental layers for the hours where bypass is systematically larger.
The tiered structure is as follows:
 • Tier1 (24-hour flat floor): The minimum hourly bypass capacity reduction across all hours
 in the window. Because the optimizer protects peak hours by concentrating bypass in off-
 peak periods, this floor is set by the peak-hour minimum and is zero for most traces.
 • Tier2 (16-hour increment): The additional capacity needed during the 16 non-peak hours
 (Mid + Off-Peak), computed as the minimum hourly bypass capacity reduction during Mid
 and Off-Peak hours minus the Tier1 floor.
 • Tier3 (8-hour increment): The additional capacity needed during the 8 Off-Peak hours
 only, computed as the minimum hourly bypass capacity reduction during Off-Peak hours
 minus the Tier1 and Tier2 levels.
Table 6.8 presents the season-spanning block for the July1–October20 analysis period. The cover-
age fraction indicates what proportion of the total bypass generation loss is addressed by the fixed
block; the remainder must be cleared in the spot market or through other procurement mechanisms.
At the P50 level, the coverage fraction is approximately 39%, indicating that the fixed block ad-
dresses roughly two-fifths of the total bypass generation loss. The remaining three-fifths varies by
day and hour and requires flexible procurement that responds to the daily and hourly variation in
bypass intensity documented in Section 4.5.
Tier 1 is zero at all percentile levels because every trace has at least some peak hours with no
bypass. This confirms that the optimizer successfully protects peak-hour generation even under
the most thermally stressed conditions, and that a 24-hour flat block cannot efficiently cover the
bypass shortfall because the shortfall is concentrated in non-peak hours.
Table 6.8: Season-spanning block purchase strategy, July 1–October 20, 2026. Tier 1 represents the mini-
mum hourly bypass capacity reduction across all hours in the window; Tier 2 and Tier 3 represent incremen-
tal capacity for the 16 non-peak and 8 off-peak hours, respectively. Coverage fraction is the ratio of energy
covered by the block purchase to total bypass generation loss.

Percentile Tier 1 (24h) Tier 2 (16h) Tier 3 (8h) Covered Total Loss Coverage

6.4.3 Single-Strategy Held Block
The single-strategy held block is sized on July 2026 conditions and held unchanged through Octo-
ber 20 (112 days). July is the conservative sizing month because its peak-tier capacity reduction is
the lowest (42.4 MW at P50, vs. 110.6 MW in August and 119.2 MW in September), so the block
does not over-purchase but substantially under-covers peak-summer months.

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

sured against the actual season-long bypass generation loss, so they reflect the cumulative shortfall
of not resizing the block month to month. At the P50 level, the peak-tier Energy Exposure is
88.0%, meaning that the July-sized peak block covers only 12% of the season-long peak-tier by-
pass generation loss. The nested-tier decomposition performs better: the Tier1 (24-hour) block at
P50 covers 36% of the season-long loss, with Tier2 and Tier3 adding incremental coverage.
This strategy is appropriate when WAPA seeks the simplest possible pre-season commitment and
plans to cover the residual shortfall through spot market purchases or other CRSP resources. The
high Energy Exposure values confirm that a held block alone is insufficient for the peak-summer
months and must be supplemented with additional procurement as the season unfolds.

6.4.4 Strategy Comparison
The three strategies represent a tradeoff between procurement complexity and shortfall coverage.
At the P50 percentile level, the monthly shaped strategy covers the full bypass shortfall within each
month by construction (0% Energy Exposure in the nested-tier total), but requires four separate
monthly procurement transactions. The season-spanning strategy covers approximately 39% of the
season-long shortfall through a single pre-season commitment, with the remaining 61% cleared
through flexible procurement. The single-strategy held block covers approximately 36% of the
season-long shortfall at the Tier 1 level, with higher residual exposure because the July-sized block
is undersized for August and September.
In cost terms, the season-spanning strategy at P50 with P50 prices costs approximately $15.5M for
the covered portion (sum of Tier1 through Tier3 costs from Table 6.8 and the corresponding spot
market costs for the uncovered portion). The monthly shaped strategy distributes costs more evenly
across the season and avoids the concentration of spot market exposure during the highest-price
months. The held block strategy has the lowest pre-season commitment cost but the highest spot
market exposure during August and September, when wholesale prices are projected to be highest
(Section 6.6).
Figure 6.2 provides a visual comparison of the three strategies for August at the P50 level. The top-
left panel shows the raw bypass capacity reduction profile by demand tier across a representative
24-hour weekday. The top-right panel decomposes the same profile into the nested-tier structure.
The bottom-left panel overlays the season-spanning block coverage on the August bypass profile,
distinguishing the covered portion (solid bars) from the residual shortfall requiring spot market
procurement (hatched areas). The bottom-right panel summarizes the season-spanning block cov-
erage as a percentage of total bypass loss by month.
The choice among strategies depends on WAPA’s procurement infrastructure, risk tolerance, and
the relative cost of forward versus spot market purchases. A combined approach is also feasi-
ble: securing a season-spanning block as the pre-season base, then supplementing with monthly

(and therefore high total generation) may appear in the upper-right quadrant, indicating that while
a large share of its release is bypassed, the absolute generation remains high because the total water
volume is large. This distinction is not visible in a one-dimensional ranking by bypass generation
loss alone.

6.6 Replacement Power Cost Estimation
The following cost estimates are planning-level indicators based on projected wholesale electric-
ity prices; actual costs will depend on market conditions, procurement strategy, and availability
of other CRSP resources. Price uncertainty is derived from the wholesale electricity price pro-
jection model of [6], which adjusts current forward prices with the distribution of historical price
premiums (Figure E.13, Appendix F). Eight price scenarios (minimum, P10, P25, P50, average,
P75, P90, maximum) are evaluated; replacement power cost is estimated as the product of hourly
bypass generation loss and the corresponding hourly price scenario.

cost table (Table 6.11) is populated by Ao using contemporaneous market price data.
