Figure 6.4 presents the overall distribution of replacement power cost outcomes across the 30-
trace ensemble and eight price scenarios. The interquartile range spans approximately $17M to
$30M, with a maximum of approximately $50M and a minimum of approximately $5M. The
central estimates based on the selected analog traces under the average price scenario suggest
a total replacement power cost of approximately $23M. This estimate represents the incremental
cost of purchasing replacement energy to cover the bypass-induced generation shortfall; it does not
include the base cost of GCD operations, which is unchanged by bypass operations. This figure is
notably higher than existing estimates in prior financial and economic assessments [8, 1], reflecting
increasingly unfavorable water-supply and hydrothermal conditions in the upper Colorado River
Basin.
The left panel of Figure 6.4 (blue violin) isolates the sensitivity of replacement power cost to hy-
drological variability. The projections do not indicate strong evidence that hydrological variability
alone drives substantial differences in replacement power cost. The right panel (yellow violin) ex-
amines the influence of price scenario realizations. The scatter points are clearly stratified by price
level, indicating that uncertainty in future electricity prices has a considerably stronger effect on
projected replacement power cost than the range of historically observed hydrological conditions.
This finding has a direct implication for replacement power planning: hydrological monitoring in-

 95

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

Figure 6.5: Distribution of monthly replacement power cost ($M) under the RM15 compliance strategy
across the 30-trace ensemble and eight price scenarios, July 10 to October 20 2026.

forms the physical volume of replacement power needed, but price risk management is the primary
cost lever.
Figure 6.5 disaggregates the results by month. The central estimates of monthly replacement power
costs are approximately 4.5M,7.2M, 6.8M, and3M for July through October, respectively. The
spread of replacement power costs is relatively narrow in June but widens markedly from July
through September. These intra-seasonal differences in uncertainty are predominantly driven by
the volatility of projected electricity prices during the summer months, rather than by hydrolog-
ical variability. From July through September, the divergence between price scenarios becomes
increasingly pronounced: higher price assumptions shift the full distribution upward and widen
the gap between the lower and upper bounds. This pattern is most evident in August and Septem-
ber, when elevated projected price levels and their associated volatility produce both the highest
projected costs and the broadest uncertainty envelope.
Removing generation of this magnitude from the Desert Southwest wholesale electricity mar-
ket during peak summer months would tend to increase regional wholesale prices as the supply-

 96

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

demand balance tightens. This coupling effect implies that the replacement power cost estimates
presented here, which use price projections that do not account for the GCD generation reduction,
likely represent a lower bound on the true costs. The magnitude of this price feedback depends on
concurrent supply and demand conditions across markets accessible to WAPA’s CRSP marketing
area and is beyond the scope of this analysis.

6.7 Monitoring Triggers and Adaptive Replacement Power Plan-
 ning
The percentile-level framework and block purchase strategies presented in the preceding sections
characterize the range of replacement power needs across the 30-trace ensemble. As the bypass
season unfolds, real-time observations of penstock temperature and bypass intensity can narrow
this range, enabling WAPA to adjust its procurement position. This section presents the monitoring
triggers, driver analysis, and conditional updating framework that support adaptive replacement
power planning.

6.7.1 Conditional Updating of Replacement Power Projections
Monthly conditioning on observed penstock temperature provides the strongest basis for mid-
season updating of replacement power projections. Table6.12 provides conditional percentiles
based on whether the observed monthly average penstock temperature falls above or below the
ensemble median for that month. The ensemble median June penstock temperature is 16.12 ◦ C. If
the observed June average exceeds this value, the conditional P50 bypass generation loss increases

 97

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

by approximately 17 GWh (from 331 to 348 GWh for the full simulation window), corresponding
to roughly $1.1M in additional replacement power cost at median prices. A warm July shifts the
conditional P50 by approximately 19 GWh.
These conditional estimates enable mid-season updating of replacement power projections as ther-
mal conditions are observed. The recommended actions in Table 6.12 illustrate how the percentile-
level framework from Section 6.1 can be operationalized through the season: a pre-season firm
base is secured at the unconditional P10 level, and successive tranches are activated as observed
conditions confirm whether the season is tracking toward the warm or cool end of the ensemble
distribution.

are split at the ensemble median monthly average penstock temperature. These conditional estimates enable
mid-season updating of replacement power projections as thermal conditions are observed.

6.7.2 Correlation and Driver Analysis
The pairwise Spearman correlation matrix (Figure E.14, Appendix F) and the top-driver analysis
(Figure6.6) identify the variables most strongly associated with bypass generation loss across the
30-trace ensemble. Season-average penstock temperature emerges as the dominant driver, consis-
tent with the thermal budget analysis in Chapter3. The PCF is the secondary driver, reflecting the
double-penalty mechanism documented in Section 5.3. Total season inflow shows a weaker but
positive correlation with bypass generation loss (FigureE.15, Appendix F), because higher inflows
tend to be associated with warmer reservoir conditions in the historical record.

Binding constraint analysis. A primal-solution-based binding constraint analysis across all 30
traces reveals the hierarchy of active constraints during the bypass season. The daily average tem-
perature constraint at RM15 is binding in 99.6–100% of hours during July through September,

 98

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

confirming that temperature management is the dominant operational constraint. The turbine min-
imum flow bound is the second most frequently binding constraint, active in 57.6% of July hours,
72.6% of August hours, and 71.8% of September hours, indicating that the optimizer frequently
operates the turbine at its minimum allowed flow to maximize bypass volume. The total flow min-
imum bound is binding in 37–42% of hours during the same period, reflecting the daily release
volume constraint. Turbine ramp-up limits are binding in 2–4% of hours, constraining the rate at
which generation can be shifted from off-peak to peak demand periods. No flow maximum bounds
are binding in any month, confirming that the system is constrained by temperature and minimum
flows rather than by maximum capacity.

6.7.3 Monitoring Triggers and Early Warning
Table 6.14 defines three monitoring zones based on weekly average penstock temperature, with
zone boundaries set at the 33rd and 67th percentiles of the weekly average across the 30-trace
ensemble. The Green zone (below 19.86◦ C) corresponds to routine monitoring conditions. The
Yellow zone (19.86–22.24◦ C) indicates heightened monitoring and preparation of contingency
procurement. The Red zone (above 22.24◦ C) triggers activation of contingency procurement.

Table 6.14: Weekly penstock temperature monitoring trigger thresholds and associated bypass loss statistics
across the 30-trace ensemble. Zone boundaries correspond to the 33rd and 67th percentiles of weekly
average penstock temperature. Weekly average penstock temperature provides limited discriminating power
for season-total outcomes. Monthly conditioning (Table 6.12) offers stronger predictive value.

The conditional season-total bypass generation loss values associated with each zone provide lim-
ited discriminating power at the weekly level: the conditional P50 shifts by only approximately
3GWh between the Green and Red zones. This modest differentiation reflects the fact that weekly
penstock temperature captures only a snapshot of thermal conditions, while season-total bypass
generation loss integrates conditions over the full bypass season. Monthly conditioning (Sec-
tion6.7.1) offers substantially stronger predictive value because it captures the sustained thermal
trajectory rather than a single-week observation.

primary monitoring variable for real-time assessment of bypass severity.

6.8 Scheduling Strategies
The dispatch optimization reveals four recurring operational patterns that arise naturally from net-
benefit maximization under the RM15 temperature constraint, all broadly consistent with current
WAPA scheduling practice.

Bypass concentration in low-demand hours - The optimization allocates bypass to the hours
of lowest net demand. Because solar penetration in the Desert Southwest has shifted the lowest
net demand hours toward midday, a substantial share of bypass is scheduled during those periods
rather than overnight. During August, the median bypass fraction during off-peak hours exceeds
the peak-hour fraction by a substantial margin. This concentration is the single most effective lever
for reducing the capacity impact of bypass operations (Figures 4.5 and 4.7).

Weekday-weekend differentiation - The optimization schedules higher bypass fractions on
weekend days, when demand is systematically lower. The weekend water volumes are derived
from CRiSPPy runs of the 24-month study and already reflect current WAPA scheduling practice.
The resulting differentiation reduces the bypass burden on high-demand weekdays without altering
total weekly release volume.

Within-week flexibility - When thermal conditions permit, the optimization concentrates bypass
in the early portion of the week and preserves flexibility for later days. When penstock tempera-
tures are expected to rise mid-week, bypass capacity is held in reserve for the warmer days that
follow (Figure 4.7).

Bypass transition behavior - Ramp penalties discourage abrupt changes in bypass flow, pro-
ducing gradual transitions between bypass and non-bypass periods. The 30-mile reach from GCD
to RM15 provides natural attenuation of associated temperature fluctuations (Appendix F, Fig-
ures E.8–E.10). The gradual-transition behavior is broadly consistent with WAPA’s operational
approach of implementing scheduling adjustments through flow pattern modifications.
These four patterns are consistent with the LTEMP ROD operational framework and do not require
modifications to existing dispatch procedures.

 103

Chapter 7

Conclusions and Recommendations

7.1 Conclusions
This report has quantified the generation and capacity cost of maintaining a daily flow-weighted
average temperature of 15.5 ◦ C at RM15 across 30 hydrological traces spanning the 1991–2020
historical record. The central finding is that RM15 compliance will consume a substantial but
highly predictable share of GCD’s generation potential during summer 2026. The predictability
of the energy impact, and the comparatively greater uncertainty of the capacity impact, together
define the planning problem that WAPA faces.

7.1.1 The Energy Dimension Is Highly Predictable
Across all four co-equal analysis periods, the coefficient of variation of bypass generation loss is
approximately 5 % (Table 5.1). This narrow spread means that once the analysis period is fixed,
the total energy shortfall is largely determined by the deterministic thermal regime rather than by
hydrological variability. The practical consequence is that WAPA can size the energy component
of replacement power with high confidence well before the bypass season begins. The P10 value of
the ensemble distribution (the level exceeded by 90 % of traces) provides a firm floor for pre-season
procurement, and the increment between P10 and P90 is modest in absolute terms, approximately
20–40 GWh depending on the analysis period (Table 2).

7.1.2 The Capacity Dimension Is Substantially More Uncertain
Peak-tier capacity reduction has a coefficient of variation of approximately 18 %, roughly 3.3 times
larger than the energy CV (Section 5.1.3). This asymmetry arises because capacity reduction de-
pends on the hour-by-hour dispatch allocation between turbines and bypass, which is sensitive to
the diurnal demand profile, the instantaneous penstock temperature, and the optimizer’s strategy

 104

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

of concentrating bypass in off-peak hours (Section 4.5). The most operationally consequential
capacity reductions occur during August and September peak hours, when the P90 peak-tier re-
duction reaches 122.6 MW and 125.2 MW respectively (Table 6.2). Because capacity shortfalls
during peak demand hours carry disproportionate consequences for firm commitment obligations,
the capacity component of replacement power should be sized more conservatively than the energy
component.

7.1.3 Penstock Temperature Governs the Bypass Regime
The thermal analysis in Chapter 3 established that penstock temperature is the dominant driver
of bypass requirements (ρ = 0.79 with total bypass generation loss; Section 6.7.2). Downstream
warming from GCD to RM15 is the secondary thermal driver, and the PCF acts as a tertiary am-
plifier through the double-penalty mechanism described in Section 5.3. This driver hierarchy has
a direct monitoring implication: weekly penstock temperature is the single most informative real-
time indicator of bypass severity, and the conditional percentile framework (Table 6.12) translates
observed temperatures into updated procurement projections.

7.1.4 Price Uncertainty Dominates Cost Uncertainty
The replacement power cost analysis in Section 6.6 demonstrated that uncertainty in future whole-
sale electricity prices exerts a considerably stronger influence on projected cost than hydrological
variability. The 30-trace ensemble produces a P10–P90 energy spread of approximately 39 GWh,
whereas the eight price scenarios span a much wider range of cost outcomes (Table 6.11). This
finding separates the planning problem into two distinct components: hydrological monitoring
informs the physical volume of replacement power needed, while price risk management is the
primary lever for controlling cost. The price projections used in this analysis do not account for
the market impact of removing 250–320 GWh from the Desert Southwest wholesale market during
peak summer months; the reported cost estimates therefore likely represent a lower bound on the
true replacement power cost.

7.1.5 The Reported Season Understates the Full Compliance Cost
All 30 traces require bypass operations beyond the October 20 reporting cutoff, with a median
last-bypass date of November 15 (Table 5.10). The median bypass generation loss occurring after
October 20 is 38.3 GWh, and an additional 24.2 GWh (median) occurs before July 1 across all
traces (Section 5.5). Together, these excluded periods represent approximately 16 % of the full-
simulation bypass generation loss and constitute costs that fall outside the primary analysis periods
but are captured in the full June–November simulation. WAPA should account for these boundary
costs when evaluating the total financial exposure of RM15 compliance.

 105

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

7.2 Recommendations
Four recommendations follow from the findings summarised above. Each is tied to a specific ana-
lytical result and is intended to be actionable within WAPA’s existing operational and procurement
framework.

7.2.1 Size Firm Capacity Commitments at the P90 Level During August and
 September
Under RM15 compliance, GCD is projected to deliver approximately 135.1 MW during August
peak hours at the P90 percentile level (Table 6.2), computed as unconstrained capacity minus
the P90 peak-hour capacity reduction. This value represents the generation level achievable in
90 % of ensemble outcomes and provides a conservative ceiling for firm peak-hour commitments
during the bypass season. The conditional percentile analysis (Table 6.12) offers a mechanism for
revising this ceiling upward if early-season penstock temperature observations confirm conditions
consistent with the favourable end of the ensemble. WAPA should use the P90 marketable capacity
as the default ceiling for August and September firm commitments, adjusting only on the basis of
observed thermal conditions.

7.2.2 Adopt a Tiered Replacement Power Procurement Strategy
The percentile-level framework in Section 6.1 and the block purchase strategies in Section 6.4
provide the analytical basis for a tiered procurement approach. The P10 bypass generation loss
(the firm base, exceeded by more than 90 % of traces) ranges from 235.9 to 298.8 GWh across the
four analysis periods (Table 3) and should be secured pre-season through forward contracts and
favorable prices (what favorable price estimates would be is not part of this analysis). The incre-
ment between P10 and P50 should be acquired conditionally in early summer as June penstock
temperature observations clarify whether the season is tracking toward the warm or cool end of
the distribution. The increment between P50 and P90 should be reserved for real-time acquisi-
tion, activated only if penstock temperatures enter the Red monitoring zone defined in Table 6.14.
This tiered structure matches procurement commitment to the progressive resolution of uncertainty
through the season.

7.2.3 Establish a Weekly Penstock Temperature Monitoring Protocol
Weekly penstock temperature is the strongest lead indicator of bypass intensity (Section 6.7.2) and
should serve as the primary real-time monitoring variable. The three monitoring zones defined in
Table 6.14, Green (below 19.86 ◦ C), Yellow (19.86–22.24 ◦ C), and Red (above 22.24 ◦ C), provide
diagnostic trigger points for escalating procurement readiness. Monthly conditioning on observed
penstock temperature (Table 6.12) offers stronger predictive value than weekly snapshots because

 106

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

it captures the sustained thermal trajectory. WAPA should implement a formal monitoring protocol
that combines weekly zone classification with monthly conditional updating of replacement power
projections.

7.2.4 Continue Off-Peak Bypass Concentration in Dispatch Scheduling
The dispatch optimisation reveals that concentrating bypass in off-peak hours is the single most
effective lever for preserving peak-hour generation capacity (Section 4.5). During August, the
median bypass fraction during off-peak hours exceeds the peak-hour fraction by a margin of ap-
proximately 32 MW in capacity terms (Table 6.1). This strategy is already embedded in the op-
timisation and is broadly consistent with current WAPA scheduling practice. The four schedul-
ing patterns identified in Section 6.8, off-peak concentration, weekday–weekend differentiation,
within-week flexibility, and gradual bypass transitions, are consistent with the LTEMP ROD oper-
ational framework and should be maintained. The representative dispatch profiles in Figure 4.13
provide planning templates for the peak-bypass weeks of the 2026 season.

7.3 Material Limitations
Several limitations may materially affect the interpretation of the reported results. These are dis-
tinguished from standard modelling approximations, which are documented in Appendix A and
have been verified to introduce negligible error (the PCF linearisation error is less than 2 %; the
bilinear product linearisation with N = 3 partitions produces observed optimality gaps typically
below 1 %).

7.3.1 Analysis Period Truncation
The primary analysis periods end at October 1 or October 20, but all 30 traces require bypass
beyond these dates (Section 5.5). The reported bypass generation loss and replacement power
cost figures therefore systematically understate the full cost of RM15 compliance. The extended-
horizon analysis quantifies the excluded costs (median 38.3 GWh after October 20; median 24.2 GWh
before July 1), but these are not included in the primary ensemble statistics. Users of this report
should add the extended-horizon values when assessing total financial exposure.

7.3.2 Demand Profile Representation
The Demand_MW values used in the dispatch optimisation represent WAPA’s typical weekly de-
mand shape for each month, which is used as a planning shape, not a firm contractual obligation.
Actual DSA commitments may differ, and other CRSP generators can partially cover any gap be-
tween GCD output and the demand shape. The capacity reduction metrics in this report measure

 107

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

bypass impact relative to unconstrained GCD potential, not relative to WAPA’s contractual obliga-
tions. A comparison against DSA capacity from CRiSPPy runs without bypass constraints would
provide a more operationally relevant baseline and represents a natural extension of this analysis.

7.3.3 Regulatory and Operational Interactions
The analysis evaluates the RM15 temperature constraint in isolation and does not account for
potential interactions with other LTEMP ROD operational requirements, including experimental
high-flow releases, sediment management flows, or minimum flow constraints that may be simul-
taneously binding. Should multiple constraints bind concurrently, the actual generation impact
could differ from the values reported here.

7.3.4 Equiprobable Trace Assumption
The 30 hydrological traces are treated as equiprobable realisations of the historical record. Boot-
strap analysis (10,000 resamples) indicates that central percentiles are estimated with ±3.8 % pre-
cision and tail percentiles with ±8.7 % precision; the 95 % confidence interval for the ensemble
median is ±3 GWh (Section 5.1.3). The central estimate is robust to all alternative trace-weighting
schemes tested (Appendix D), but the tails of the distribution are less precisely characterised and
should be interpreted with appropriate caution.

7.4 Future Work
Three analytical enhancements are recommended for subsequent analyses. First, integration of
SPP demand curves in place of the current historical load profile would improve the realism of the
demand-tier classification and the capacity reduction estimates. Second, expanded price scenario
analysis using contemporaneous forward market data would narrow the cost projection uncertainty,
which this analysis has identified as the dominant source of financial risk. Third, investigation of a
quadratic programming formulation would eliminate the bilinear linearisation approximation used
for the temperature mixing constraint (Appendix A) and serve as a benchmark for assessing the
linearisation error.
After the 2026 bypass season concludes, actual bypass volumes, generation impacts, and replace-
ment power costs should be compared against the projections in this report. This post-season
retrospective will validate the modelling approach, identify systematic biases in the thermal or
hydrological inputs, and inform calibration adjustments for future analyses. Particular attention
should be given to the accuracy of the penstock temperature profiles, the downstream warming
lookup table, and the PCF approximation, as these are the three inputs with the greatest influence
on bypass generation loss projections.

 108

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

Based on stakeholder feedback, future analyses will explore multi-percentile heatmaps and inter-
active visualization tools that allow users to construct thermal and dispatch diagnostics at user-
selected percentile or exceedance levels.
A comparison of RM15 compliance generation impacts against a CRiSPPy normal-operations
baseline (without bypass constraints) would provide a more operationally relevant reference for
capacity planning and is recommended for future analyses.

Appendix A

Mathematical Formulation

This appendix provides the complete mathematical formulation of the MILP dispatch optimiza-
tion model. The physical system, key definitions, derived metrics, and solver configuration are
presented in Chapter 2. The notation follows the conventions established in Section 2.1; gauge-
location variables (qgg , Tgg ) denote the downstream compliance monitoring point and are used
interchangeably with the total-flow and mixed-temperature variables at that location.

A.1 Objective Function
The model maximizes net revenue over all plants p ∈ P and hours t ∈ T :
  
 max ∑ ∑ Rev(p,t) − RampPen(p,t) − ∑ SlackPen(p) (A.1)
 p∈P t∈T p∈P

Revenue term. Hourly revenue is the product of turbine power output and the locational marginal
price at the corresponding network node:

                          Rev(p,t) = ∑ ptur (p,t) · LMP(n,t) · A(p, n)                       (A.2)
 n∈N

where A(p, n) ∈ {0, 1} is the plant-to-node allocation factor indicating which node plant p serves.
In this formulation, “revenue” refers to the value of generation computed as power output times
the locational marginal price; it serves as the objective function metric and does not represent
commercial market revenue.

 113

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

Ramp penalty terms. Ramp penalties discourage rapid changes in bypass, turbine, total, and
gauge flows:

 RampPen(p,t) = Cbp,up · qbp,ramp_up (p,t) +Cbp,dn · qbp,ramp_down (p,t)
 +Ctur,up · qtur,ramp_up (p,t) +Ctur,dn · qtur,ramp_down (p,t)
 +Ctot,up · qtot,ramp_up (p,t) +Ctot,dn · qtot,ramp_down (p,t)
 +Cgg,up · qgg,ramp_up (p,t) +Cgg,dn · qgg,ramp_down (p,t) (A.3)

where C∗,up and C∗,dn are penalty coefficients in $/CFS for each flow type.

Slack penalty terms. Slack penalties impose large costs on violations of daily release targets,
cyclic continuity, and power demand balance, ensuring these soft constraints are satisfied in prac-
tice:
 "
 
 SlackPen(p) = Cslack ∑ stot,d,min (p, d) + stot,d,max (p, d)
 d∈D
 + sbp,cyc,min (p) + sbp,cyc,max (p) + stur,cyc,min (p) + stur,cyc,max (p)
 #
 + stot,cyc,min (p) + stot,cyc,max (p) + sgg,cyc,min (p) + sgg,cyc,max (p)
 
 +Cslack ∑ ∑ snode,min(n,t) + snode,max(n,t) (A.4)
 n∈N t∈T

where Cslack is a large penalty coefficient (in $/CFS or $/MW) that makes slack activation pro-
hibitively expensive under normal operating conditions.
Slack variables are introduced for constraints that may be infeasible under certain combinations of
release targets and temperature limits. The penalty coefficients are set sufficiently high to ensure
that slack is used only when strict feasibility is unattainable; total slack usage across all traces is
monitored as a model diagnostic.

A.2 Constraints
A.2.1 Release Composition
At every hour, the total release is the sum of turbine and bypass flows:

                         qbp (p,t) + qtur (p,t) = qtot (p,t) ∀ p ∈ P, t ∈ T                            (A.5)

This identity links the two release pathways and is the foundation of the temperature mixing model
(Equation 2.2).

 114

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

A.2.2 Daily Release Volume Targets
Trace-specific daily total release volumes are enforced as equality constraints:

                          ∑ qtot(p,t) = Qtot,daily(p, d) ∀ p ∈ P, d ∈ D                          (A.6)
 t∈T (d)

where T (d) denotes the 24 hourly periods belonging to day d, and Qtot,daily (p, d) is the target daily
release volume (in CFS-hours, converted from monthly acre-foot targets). Slack variables stot,d,min
and stot,d,max soften this constraint when necessary.

A.2.3 Ramping Rate Constraints
Ramp magnitudes are defined by the hour-to-hour change in each flow variable. For bypass flow:

                        qbp (p,t) − qbp (p,t − 1) ≤ qbp,ramp_up (p,t) ∀ p, t                     (A.7)
                        qbp (p,t − 1) − qbp (p,t) ≤ qbp,ramp_down (p,t) ∀ p, t                   (A.8)

Analogous constraints apply for turbine, total, and gauge flows. Ramp magnitudes are bounded
above by flow-type-specific limits:

 qbp,ramp_up (p,t) ≤ Qbp,ramp_up,max (p) ∀ p, t (A.9)
 qbp,ramp_down (p,t) ≤ Qbp,ramp_down,max (p) ∀ p, t (A.10)

These constraints, combined with the ramp penalty terms in the objective (Equation A.3), discour-
age abrupt changes in release flows.

A.2.4 Cyclic Continuity
Within each weekly window, hour 1 is linked to hour 168 via slack variables to encourage (but not
rigidly enforce) continuity:

                qbp (p, 0) + sbp,cyc,min (p) = qbp (p, 167) + sbp,cyc,max (p) ∀ p ∈ P           (A.11)

Analogous constraints apply for turbine, total, and gauge flows. The slack variables absorb any
week-boundary discontinuity at a high penalty cost.

 115

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

A.2.5 Daily Operating Range
Hourly total flow is bounded within a daily operating range to reflect operational ramp-down and
ramp-up limits across the day:

 Qtot,min (p, d) ≤ qtot (p,t) ≤ Qtot,min (p, d) + Qmax,daily_change (p, d) ∀ p, d, t ∈ T (d) (A.12)

where Qtot,min (p, d) is the daily minimum total flow and Qmax,daily_change (p, d) is the maximum
allowable intra-day variation.

A.2.6 Power Conversion
Turbine power output is a linear function of turbine flow scaled by the power conversion factor:

                        ptur (p,t) = qtur (p,t) · PCF(p,t) · α    ∀ p ∈ P, t ∈ T                     (A.13)

where PCF(p,t) is the power conversion factor (MW/AFH) at the prevailing pool elevation (Sec-
tion 2.3) and α is the unit conversion constant from CFS to AFH. This linearisation introduces
an approximation error of less than 2% relative to the physical turbine performance curve (Fig-
ure A.2).

A.2.7 Power Demand Balance
Generation at each network node must match the hourly demand profile, subject to slack:

 Demand(n,t) + snode,min (n,t) − snode,max (n,t) = ∑ ptur (p,t) · A(p, n) ∀ n ∈ N , t ∈ T (A.14)
 p∈P

where Demand(n,t) is the hourly load at node n and A(p, n) is the plant-to-node allocation factor.
The slack variables snode,min and snode,max allow the constraint to be violated at high cost.

A.2.8 Temperature Mixing (Bilinear Linearization)
The outlet temperature at GCD is determined by the energy balance of mixed flows (Equation 2.2).
In the MILP, the bilinear product qgg (p,t)·Tgg (p,t) is replaced by the latent variable QT_latent(p,t),
which is bounded by a sum-of-convex linearization (see Section A.3). The mixing balance is then
enforced as:

    qtur (p,t) · Ttur (p,t) + qbp (p,t) · Tbp (t) + QT_latent(p,t) = qtot (p,t) · Tgg (p,t) ∀ p, t   (A.15)

Note that Ttur (p,t) and Tbp (t) are input parameters (not decision variables), so the products qtur ·Ttur
and qbp · Tbp are linear in the flow variables.

 116

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

A.2.9 Hourly Temperature Bounds
The gauge temperature at each location is bounded hourly to enforce outlet temperature limits:

      qtur (p,t) · Ttur (p,t) + qbp (p,t) · Tbp (t) + QT_latent(p,t) ≤ Tgg,max (p,t) · qtot (p,t)    (A.16)
      qtur (p,t) · Ttur (p,t) + qbp (p,t) · Tbp (t) + QT_latent(p,t) ≥ Tgg,min (p,t) · qtot (p,t)    (A.17)

for all p ∈ P, t ∈ T . These constraints bound the hourly mixed outlet temperature between Tgg,min
and Tgg,max .

A.2.10 Daily Average Temperature Constraint
The primary compliance constraint requires that the flow-weighted daily average temperature at
RM15 not exceed Ttarget = 15.5 ◦ C. To avoid division in the MILP, the constraint is reformulated
as:
 h i
  ∑ turq (p,t)·Ttur (p,t)+q bp (p,t)·Tbp (t)+QT_latent(p,t) ≤ Tgg,daily (p, d)· ∑ qtot (p,t) ∀ p, d
t∈T (d)                                                                                   t∈T (d)
 (A.18)
where Tgg,daily (p, d) is the daily temperature limit at RM15, set to Ttarget − ∆T (d, q̄tot ) after back-
calculation from the downstream warming lookup table (Equation 2.3).
When the input day-of-year and total flow fall between grid points of the lookup table, bilinear
interpolation is used to estimate ∆T .

A.2.11 Rough Zone Constraints
When enabled, turbine flow is constrained to operate within defined operating regions, avoiding
rough zones that cause mechanical wear. The commitment variable utur (p, r,t) selects the active
rough zone:

                             ∑ utur(p, r,t) · Qrz,min(p, r) ≤ qtur(p,t) ∀ p, t                       (A.19)
 r

                            ∑ utur(p, r,t) · Qrz,max(p, r) ≥ qtur(p,t) ∀ p, t                        (A.20)
 r

                                            ∑ utur(p, r,t) ≤ 1 ∀ p, t                                (A.21)
 r

where Qrz,min (p, r) and Qrz,max (p, r) are the lower and upper flow bounds for rough zone r at plant
p.

 117

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

A.2.12 Pattern Repeat Constraints
When enabled, days belonging to the same day-group (e.g., all Mondays within the weekly win-
dow) are constrained to have identical hourly release patterns:

 qbp (p,td ) = qbp (p,td ′ ) if d and d ′ belong to the same day-group (A.22)

for all p ∈ P. Analogous constraints apply for turbine and total flows. This constraint reduces the
effective number of free variables and can improve solver performance when weekly patterns are
expected to repeat.

A.3 Bilinear Linearization Details
The temperature mixing constraint (Equation 2.2) introduces a bilinear product Z = X · Y , where
X = qgg (p,t) (gauge flow, CFS) and Y = Tgg (p,t) (gauge temperature, ◦ C). This product is re-
placed by the latent variable Z = QT_latent(p,t) and approximated using a sum-of-convex lin-
earization with N = 3 partitions.

Decomposition. The domain of X is partitioned into three disjoint intervals [Xmin , X1 ], [X1 , X2 ],
[X2 , Xmax ], and the latent variable is decomposed as:

                                           Z = Z1 + Z2 + Z3                                      (A.23)

where each Zk is a piecewise-linear approximation of X ·Y over the k-th interval. Each component
Zk is bounded by a set of linear inequalities derived from the McCormick envelope applied to the
restricted sub-domain:

 Zk ≥ Xkmin ·Y +Ymin · Xk − Xkmin ·Ymin (A.24)
 Zk ≥ Xkmax ·Y +Ymax · Xk − Xkmax ·Ymax (A.25)
 Zk ≤ Xkmax ·Y +Ymin · Xk − Xkmax ·Ymin (A.26)
 Zk ≤ Xkmin ·Y +Ymax · Xk − Xkmin ·Ymax (A.27)

where [Xkmin , Xkmax ] are the bounds of the k-th sub-interval and [Ymin ,Ymax ] are the global bounds on
temperature.

Tightness relative to a single McCormick envelope. A single McCormick envelope applied
over the full domain [Xmin , Xmax ] × [Ymin ,Ymax ] produces a convex relaxation whose feasible region
is a tetrahedron. By partitioning the X-domain into N = 3 sub-intervals and applying a separate
McCormick envelope to each, the sum-of-convex approach produces a tighter relaxation: the fea-

 118

 Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

 sible region of the linearized product is the intersection of three smaller tetrahedra, which more
 closely approximates the true bilinear surface. This tighter relaxation reduces the optimality gap
 and improves solver convergence compared to the single-envelope approach.

 Bounds. The bounds used in the linearization are:
 • Xmin , Xmax : lower and upper bounds on gauge flow qgg (CFS), derived from the monthly
 flow limits.
 • Ymin , Ymax : lower and upper bounds on gauge temperature Tgg (◦ C), derived from the range
 of bypass and turbine temperatures across all traces.
 The partition points X1 and X2 divide the flow domain into three equal-width sub-intervals. The
 resulting linearization is embedded in the MILP as the set of constraints (A.24), with the decom-
 position identity (A.23) enforced as an equality. This approach is implemented in the tempregpy
 package (see also Equation 2.2).

Appendix D

Reference Trace Selection Details

This appendix describes the two-tier trace selection framework used to identify traces for detailed
illustration and planning context throughout the report.
Five reference traces are selected at fixed percentile positions of the bypass generation loss distribu-
tion, providing statistical anchors for dispatch profile illustrations and detailed analyses. Selection
is deterministic: all 30 traces are ranked by total bypass generation loss over the July 1 through
October 20 analysis period, and the traces at ranks 3 (P10), 8 (P25), 15 (P50), 23 (P75), and 28
(P90) are designated as reference traces.

Table D.1: Tier 1 reference traces at fixed percentile positions of the 30-trace bypass generation loss distri-
bution.

Percentile Trace Bypass Loss (GWh) Total Gen (GWh) Peak Cap Red (MW)
P10           X2011               298.8                    387.83                      64.5
P25           X1997               309.6                    389.44                      70.1
P50           X2013               318.3                    370.66                      78.1
P75           X2020               324.8                    357.11                      83.2
P90           X2018               337.5                    385.40                      81.2

Six analog traces are selected using a weighted Euclidean distance metric that identifies the his-
torical years most closely resembling projected 2026 conditions from the May 2026 24-Month
Study (Run ID 3317). The distance metric incorporates end-of-month pool elevation (weight 0.5),
monthly inflow volume (weight 0.3), and monthly release volume (weight 0.2), each normalized
by the ensemble standard deviation and summed over the June through October period. The six
analog traces are X2011, X2015, X2014, X2007, X2008, and X2001.
A sensitivity analysis evaluated the robustness of the Most Probable analog selection to alternative

 130

Glen Canyon Dam – SMB RM15 Generation Impact Assessment Summer 2026

weight specifications. The baseline weight scheme (elevation 0.5, inflow 0.3, release 0.2) retains
all six traces under elevation-heavy weighting (6/6 overlap), and five of six under equal weighting
and inflow-heavy weighting. Elevation-only and inflow-only weightings each retain four of the six
baseline analogs.

Table D.3: Sensitivity of Most Probable analog trace selection to alternative weighting schemes. Overlap is
measured against the baseline selection (elevation 0.5, inflow 0.3, release 0.2). The baseline Most Probable
analog set is X2011, X2015, X2014, X2007, X2008, X2001.

 Overlap with
 Weight Scheme Top-6 Analog Traces
 Baseline
Baseline (0.5/0.3/0.2)               X2011, X2015, X2014, X2007, X2008, X2001                      6/6
Equal (0.333 each)                   X2015, X2014, X2011, X2007, X2008, X2003                      5/6
Elevation heavy (0.7/0.2/0.1)        X2011, X2014, X2007, X2015, X2001, X2008                      6/6
Inflow heavy (0.2/0.6/0.2)           X2015, X2011, X2014, X2007, X2008, X2012                      5/6
Elevation only                       X2004, X2011, X2014, X2007, X2001, X2019                      4/6
Inflow only                          X2015, X2011, X2014, X2010, X2012, X2001                      4/6

The Most Probable analog set (X2011, X2015, X2014, X2007, X2008, X2001) was selected by
the multi-variable distance algorithm using the baseline weight scheme (elevation 0.5, inflow 0.3,
release 0.2). One trace (X2011) appears in both the analog and reference sets, confirming that the
closest analog to the Most Probable 24MS scenario also falls near the P10 percentile of the bypass
generation loss distribution. The analog set spans ensemble ranks 2 to 27 of 30, providing broad
coverage of the plausible outcome space.
The correlation between season-average penstock temperature and total bypass generation loss
(ρ = 0.79; Section 6.7.2) confirms that elevation is a reasonable proxy for the primary bypass
driver. K-means clustering (K = 4) assigns the six Most Probable analogs to two of the four
clusters, confirming that the distance-based selection captures a coherent subset of the ensemble.

D.1 Min Probable Analog Selection
Six analog traces are also identified for the 24MS Min Probable scenario, which reflects drier-than-
expected inflow conditions. Using the same weighted Euclidean distance algorithm with the Min
Probable inflow target, the six selected Min Probable analogs are: X2012, X2008, X2001, X2019,
X2017, and X2003. Two traces (X2008 and X2001) appear in both the Most Probable and Min
Probable analog sets, indicating that these years represent conditions intermediate between the two
forecast scenarios.
A sensitivity analysis of the Min Probable analog selection to alternative weight specifications
is shown in Table D.5. The baseline selection is robust: five of six traces are retained under

Appendix G

Date Sensitivity Analysis

Table G.1 quantifies the bypass generation loss avoided by alternative start and end dates relative to the full
theoretical bypass season. The savings calculation assumes temperature compliance would not be main-
tained during excluded periods; this is a policy choice with ecological implications beyond the scope of this
report.

Table G.1: Bypass generation loss sensitivity to analysis period start and end dates. Values show median
bypass generation loss (GWh) across the 30-trace ensemble, with P10–P90 range in parentheses. “Savings”
indicates the reduction in reported bypass loss relative to the full biological season (onset to cessation).
Bypass Duration shows the median (P10–P90) across the 30-trace ensemble for each window.

 End: October 1 End: October 20
 Bypass Dur. Bypass Dur.
Start Date Loss (GWh) Loss (GWh)
 (days) (days)

Biological onset (∼Jun 9)     289.6 (273.0–305.9)       116 (109–118)     341.2 (318.9–365.9)       135 (128–137)
July 1                        265.0 (253.8–275.8)             93          318.3 (298.8–337.5)            112
July 10                       245.9 (235.9–255.1)             84          298.9 (281.0–317.9)            103

Full simulation (Jun 1–Nov 30): P50 = 381.2 (351.2–412.4) GWh

Note: Bypass Duration is fixed at 93, 112, 84, and 103 days for the Jul 1/Oct 1, Jul 1/Oct 20, Jul 10/Oct 1, and
Jul 10/Oct 20 windows, respectively (all traces share the same calendar window). For the biological onset scenarios,
duration varies across traces; median and P10–P90 range are shown. Bypass generation loss P10–P90 ranges for the
biological onset rows are derived from the full ensemble distribution.

The end date has a larger impact on reported bypass loss than the start date. Moving the end date from
October 20 to October 1 reduces reported loss by approximately 54 GWh (median), while delaying the start
from July 1 to July 10 reduces reported loss by approximately 20 GWh.
